Suspending sets Supabase Auth's own ban on the account, which refuses every way of signing in and every token refresh, deletes every session so the person is signed out everywhere at once, and the database's request check refuses any session of a banned account, even one begun a moment before. Signing in, they are told the account is suspended and how to reach support. Reactivating lifts the ban. Resetting two-step verification removes every authenticator and signs the person out everywhere, so a school owner or staff member sets it up again at their next sign-in (AUTH-08). Suspending an account leaves their Business as it is: to stop its bookings, staff suspend the Business (D-125). Suspensions are kept in a table only staff read, because colleagues can read each other's rows in `users`.
- **Options:** The Auth admin API with the secret key, from the server; a flag the app's own gates check; letting support staff reset two-step verification.
- **Reason:** The ban is the column Auth itself checks at sign-in and at refresh, so one database function covers the password, links, codes, Google and Apple without the secret key leaving the webhook and the job runner, and deleting sessions ends access at once as signing a device out already does (D-041). A flag in the app alone would leave sign-in working and a token valid against the database API for up to an hour. Resetting two-step verification is how accounts are taken over by somebody who talks their way past support, so it stays with the founders, and the question they are asked says to check the request comes from the person.

## D-127 | 2026-09-17 | How regions are counted, and what opening one does
- **Decision:** An instructor covers a postcode area when search may show them (checked, badge in date, listed, still at a Business in good standing) and they are based in the area or add a district in it; how far their radius reaches into a neighbouring area is not counted. Their free hours are their open time in the next 14 days (working hours and extra hours, less time off) that no lesson, its buffer or a request still waiting takes, shown in whole hours rounded down. Demand is the addresses on the area's waiting list and its open lesson requests. The Regions screen lists every area with an instructor, somebody waiting, a request, or a past switch, those ready to open first. Support staff see it; only a super admin opens or closes an area (PRD 6.2). An area opens only when it meets the switch-on rule, whose numbers are the platform settings (M5-20 edits them); closing is always allowed, and an open area that later falls short stays open until somebody closes it. Both are audited with the area's counts at that moment. Opening enqueues an event, and the job emails each address waiting in the area once, however many entries it has there, under a key of its own so a retry sends nothing twice; the waiting-list place is then left, its job done, and a lesson request is kept and marked as told. Nobody is told about an area closed again before the job runs, and opening an area again tells nobody twice. The email points to the learners page's instructor finder, since Phase 1 has no marketplace search yet.
- **Options:** Count radius coverage from district centroids; let a super admin open an area short of the rule; keep waiting-list places after telling them; send one email per entry.
- **Reason:** Where an instructor is based and the districts they add are what they chose and what their profile shows, so every count can be explained area by area; centroids for every postcode district would be a new dataset kept for a switch Phase 3 depends on. PRD 4.2 makes the rule the condition for opening, and the numbers are already the super admin's to change. The consent was to be emailed once when the area opens, so keeping the address afterwards serves nothing, while a lesson request is demand data a learner can still withdraw. One email to an address keeps somebody with a place on the list and a request from being told twice.

## D-128 | 2026-09-17 | Which platform settings a super admin changes, and how
- **Decision:** The Settings screen changes five settings, each kept in `platform_settings`: the default booking rules every Business starts with (the gap between lessons, notice, how far ahead, the cancellation window, the late fee, how long a request waits, and reminder times); the regional switch-on rule; plan limits, which today means the text message reminders Pro and School include a month; the marketplace fee, kept for Phase 3 and charged by nothing yet; and feature flags (Google sign-in, Apple sign-in, marketplace search). Support staff see them read-only; only a super admin past their second step saves one, through a database function that checks the whole value against the same bounds the product enforces elsewhere and audits the change with what it was and what it became. Saving the default booking rules has every profile's free times read again, and saving the flags has every page read them again at once; sign-in and sign-up read the flags on the server, kept for minutes. The jobs that send texts now read the plan limits through a `system_` function, falling back to the plan's own allowance. The default reminder times had been stored under a name nothing read, so every reminder used the times in code; the migration moves them to the name a Business uses, and the reminder job falls back to them. Nobody signed in can write to `platform_settings` directly. The founding offer and plan prices are not changed here: they are what the pricing page promises (PRD 9.18), and plans and subscriptions are ADM-10, Phase 2.
- **Options:** A `feature_flags` table as `packages/config/src/flags.ts` once anticipated; editing the founding offer and plan prices here too; saving each field as it changes; leaving plan limits in code.
- **Reason:** One row for each kind of setting keeps a single audited function and one set of checks, and the flags need no table of their own for three yes or no values. The founding offer's numbers already drive who gets it in the database, and changing them here without the pricing page changing with them would break a public promise, so they wait for the plans work that changes both together. A save for each setting lets the database check the whole value at once, which a save per field could not. The text limit is the one plan limit the product enforces today, and an allowance that only a deploy can change is not a setting.

## D-129 | 2026-09-17 | Viewing as somebody is the database's, and read only there
- **Decision:** Platform staff past their second step, support and super admins alike, view as anybody who is not platform staff, from that person's panel, saying why. The database records the viewing against the staff member's own Auth session, and the start and the end go in the audit log with the reason. The viewing's id is kept in a cookie the page can read and sent with every database request, from the server and from the browser, as `x-view-as`. The request check that already runs before every database request (D-041) honours it only from the session that started the viewing, while it runs (30 minutes at most) and while the staff member is still staff past their second step. It then runs the rest of the request as the person viewed, so every screen and every rule shows exactly what they see, in a read-only transaction, so the database refuses any write from any screen, whatever function it goes through. The app shows a yellow banner on every portal screen with Stop viewing; stopping goes back to the admin portal and ends the viewing from the staff member's own requests. A viewing that has ended sends the browser to forget it. The person's own account, devices and two-step verification pages are not opened while viewing, and their second step is not asked for. Actions that reach the payments provider before the database refuse outright, because a read-only database stops the write only after a card was charged. Nothing is kept on the device for no signal while viewing, and back in the admin portal anything their screens left is removed.
- **Options:** Sign in as the person with a token minted from the project's signing key; a separate read-only copy of each screen built for staff; staff policies on every table instead of running as the person; letting support staff start viewing only with a super admin's approval.
- **Reason:** A token minted from the signing key is the service key's power in the web server, which the security rules keep out of it, and it could write. Copies of screens drift from what people actually see, which is the whole point of looking. Running as the person in the database gives the same answer the person gets, and the read-only transaction is enforced by Postgres itself rather than by every function remembering to check. PRD ADM-06 gives the feature to support, and the audit log with its reason is the control; an approval step would slow the support calls it exists for.

## D-130 | 2026-09-17 | What the audit log shows staff, and how they look through it
- **Decision:** Platform staff past their second step, support and super admins alike, read the audit log in the admin portal, newest first, 50 entries to a page with Older entries and Newest entries. Each entry says when it happened in London, with the year; what happened in words, with the recorded action under it; who did it and as what (their staff role, their role at the Business, their own account, or the platform itself for what no person did); whom it was about; and at which Business, with what changed behind a disclosure. Whom it was about is the person an account, staff, membership or instructor entry names, or the learner a lesson, lesson record, payment, refund, receipt or credit is for, while that row still exists. Staff narrow it by kind, by part of a name or email (of who did it, or whom it was about), by part of a Business's name, and by London days from and to. The kinds are the eight NFR-SEC-06 names first (sign-ins, role changes, verification decisions, refunds, payout and payment changes, data exports, account deletions, viewing as somebody), then platform staff decisions, lessons, and Businesses and learners. The filters are the page's address, so a search can be shared or gone back to, and one that does not read as a filter is ignored rather than refused. A page starts after the exact moment and id of the last entry shown, so entries written in the same moment are neither skipped nor shown twice. Every action a migration records has words and a kind, which a test that reads the migrations keeps true; one without words would show as recorded. Reading the log changes nothing and is not itself recorded. The shared select became a client component, because in a form drawn on the server its field labelled the box around the select rather than the select, which the accessibility scan caught.
- **Options:** A filter for each of the 66 recorded actions; numbered pages; recording every look at the log; searching inside what changed; the log for super admins only.
- **Reason:** Staff look for a kind of thing, not an action code, and NFR-SEC-06's list is the one they are asked about; the recorded action stays on each entry for precision. Numbered pages repeat or skip entries as new ones arrive, which in a log written all day is the usual case. Looking at the log changes nothing and needs the second step already, and recording each look would bury the entries that matter under entries about reading them. Searching inside what changed would need the whole log's values indexed, where name, Business, kind and day answer the questions support is asked. ADM-07 gives the log to platform staff, and support need it to answer who changed something.

## D-131 | 2026-09-17 | Another Business's learner is not found, and nobody is added to a Business by their id
- **Decision:** A learner somebody may not see is not found wherever a learner is asked for by id, and the answer is the one for an id that belongs to nobody. The policies already returned nothing from every table and view. The two functions that read one learner by id, their history and their balance, said not allowed and now say not found. `GET /api/v1/lesson-records` now answers 404 for a learner the reader may not see, where it sent an empty page before (this amends D-103); a learner of theirs with no records yet still gets an empty page. Adding a learner by hand now takes only the account the app has just made for them: one nobody has claimed (no confirmed email or mobile, never signed in) and that no Business has yet. Before, the database took any account id, so a Business holding another Business's learner's id could add that learner to itself through the API, overwrite their postcode and transmission, and then read their name, email and mobile. The app never did this, since it only passes the account it has just created (D-068), but the database must not depend on the app. acceptance-07 uses the real sessions of a school owner past her second step and of an independent instructor. It asks the database's own API over HTTP for a learner of a school the test makes, through 26 tables and views and the two functions, and asks the app's records route too. Each answers not found, exactly as for an id that belongs to nobody, and the same requests find a learner of the owner's own school. Writes that name a learner, such as booking one or changing their status, keep their refusals, which are already the same for another Business's learner as for nobody.
- **Options:** Keep not allowed, which gives away no more than not found; a learner endpoint in `/api/v1` built for the test; marking each account the app makes with who made it and checking that mark; refusing only learners another Business already has.
- **Reason:** The PRD's test asks for not found, and the pages already show Page not found for a learner who is not yours. The database's API is the one every client talks to with a person's session, so it is the one the test attacks; an endpoint built only to be tested would have no user. A mark on each account would have to be agreed between the identity module and every test's accounts, whereas unclaimed and unlinked is exactly the state D-068 leaves a new learner in, and nobody else is in it for longer than the moment between the account and its link. Refusing claimed accounts too keeps D-068's promise that nobody is attached to an account without accepting a link.

## D-132 | 2026-09-17 | Lighthouse on the public pages, and what it found
- **Decision:** Lighthouse CI (`@lhci/cli`, pinned) runs in a CI job of its own on every push, against a production build and the seeded database. It measures an instructor's profile, a school's profile, a city page, an area page and a city's automatic page, three times each, on the phone Lighthouse emulates by default over a slow 4G connection, with the user agent PageSpeed Insights sends. The median of each page's three scores must be 90 or more for performance, accessibility, best practices and SEO, and the job fails otherwise. The reports are kept with the run rather than uploaded to Lighthouse's public report storage, and the scores are written to the job's summary. The "page is crawlable" audit is left out: outside production every page asks not to be crawled (D-047), and a place page with fewer than three instructors asks not to be indexed (M5-07). Both are on purpose and tested in `seo.spec.ts`, and every other SEO audit still counts. Measuring found one real problem: a public profile loaded the whole interactive map library to show where lessons start. That cost about a quarter of its performance score, and the library's worker broke the report-only content security policy. Public pages now show the area as a Mapbox static image, with the circle and middle in the brand's colours, as ARCHITECTURE 13 intended; the interactive map stays on the instructor's own screens. In CI the profile and school pages also scored in the 70s on some runs, for a layout shift of 0.34 and 0.31, the same each time it happened. Those pages are drawn with a short skeleton before their content streams in, and when the browser painted in between, the site's footer was in view and was pushed away when the content landed. The page skeletons are now at least a screen tall, and the free times' skeleton is the size of the times it stands for. (Suspecting the typeface first, Inter was briefly set not to swap in late; the shift carried on, and the change was undone.) The assertion takes the median of each category's three scores, since Lighthouse CI's `median-run` judges every category on one run picked by its paint timings, which let those scores through. It also found every page at 86 for SEO for want of a description. Next.js puts titles and descriptions in the head for search engines, link previews and Lighthouse (whose user agent says so when PageSpeed Insights runs it), and streams them into the body for everybody else, and Lighthouse CI's own runs do not say so. The runs now send PageSpeed Insights' user agent, so they see what search engines see. With both settled, locally the five pages score 96 to 99 for performance and 100 for the other three. Largest Contentful Paint under the slow 4G simulation is 2.1 to 2.8 seconds, because a page's heading and first paragraph arrive in the part of the page streamed after its skeleton. NFR-PERF-01's 2.0 seconds on 4G is therefore not shown by this measurement, and is left for M6.
- **Options:** Measure as a desktop instead of a phone; put titles and descriptions in the head for every browser (`htmlLimitedBots` matching everything); load the interactive map only when it scrolls into view; run Lighthouse inside the end to end job; upload the reports to Lighthouse's temporary public storage.
- **Reason:** Learners find instructors on their phones, and the phone measurement is the stricter one. A picture answers the only question that section asks, how far an instructor goes, for one small image; loading the library on scroll would still send it to every phone that scrolls. Titles and descriptions in the head for every browser were tried. They scored the same, but broke Next.js's development check on every public page ("did not produce a static shell", which Next.js calls its own bug), and made every visitor wait for them, where the framework already serves the head to the crawlers the SEO score stands for. A job of its own measures pages as the seed made them, not as the end to end tests left them. Public report storage would publish the pages it measured.
- **Owner's answer, 17 September 2026:** Largest Contentful Paint of 2.1 to 2.7 seconds in Lighthouse's slow 4G simulation is accepted for now, so it is not carried into M6 as work of its own.

## D-133 | 2026-09-17 | The admin Bookings and Payments screens wait for a decision
- **Decision:** PRD 8.2 lists Bookings and Payments in the Super Admin menu, but no ADM requirement says what they hold and the plan gives them no task. Their placeholders said they would arrive in M5, which was not true. They stay in the menu, as 8.2 has them, and now say they are not built yet. They point staff to the way they can already look into somebody's lessons and payments: find the person and view as them (D-129). The product owner is asked what the two screens should hold, and when.
- **Options:** Take them out of the menu until they are specified; build lists of every booking and payment on the platform, with filters, now.
- **Reason:** Screens nobody has specified are likely to be the wrong screens. Taking them out of the menu would drop what the PRD lists. Honest placeholders cost nothing, and one decision changes them.
- **Owner's answer, 17 September 2026:** leave both screens as they are, saying they are not built and pointing staff to viewing as the person. They are looked at again in Phase 2.

## D-134 | 2026-09-17 | The move from the public site to the app is temporary
- **Decision:** An app address opened on `doovor.com` still moves to `app.doovor.com`, path and query kept, but as a temporary move rather than a permanent one (D-084 amended). A public page opened on the app still moves to the public site for good, since that is where search should find it (D-109).
- **Options:** Keep both moves permanent; keep the move permanent and rely on each release to change what caches hold.
- **Reason:** The list of public pages grows: M5 added instructor profiles, school pages and place pages, and Phase 2 adds guides and FAQs. A browser or a cache that remembered a permanent move would keep sending a page that has since become public to the app, where the app sends it back, and the two would bounce. It looked like that on staging while the new deployment caught up. Nothing on the app is indexed, so a temporary move costs no ranking; a public page's own move stays permanent, which is the one search engines read.

## D-135 | 2026-09-17 | What the OWASP review changed
- **Decision:** Three things came out of reading the code against the OWASP Top 10 (M6-01, NFR-SEC-03), all closed here. First, the levers under `/dev`, which run the jobs, charge lessons the day before, finish a fake payments account and have the public pages read fresh, refused only in production. That left them answering on staging, which has a job runner, real providers and an address anybody can reach, with nobody signed in. They now answer only where the whole environment is ours: a developer's machine and the end to end runs (`devRoutesOpen`, `APP_ENV` of `local` or `test`). Second, the job runner's signing key was required in production only; any hosted environment now requires it, so a jobs endpoint can never answer a stranger. Third, the share image drawer fetched whatever photo address it was given. The address is always built from our own storage, but an instructor may write their own photo path, so the drawer now fetches only a stored picture of ours or a picture carried in the address itself, and never follows a redirect. The review itself, with what proves each claim, is `docs/SECURITY.md`.
- **Options:** Leave the `/dev` levers for staging and rely on nobody finding them; put them behind a shared secret; leave the signing key to production and trust the hosting to be configured; check the photo's address where it is built rather than where it is fetched.
- **Reason:** An endpoint that charges a lesson or runs a job with nobody signed in should not exist where real providers are connected, and a secret to protect it would be one more thing to keep. The test runs and a developer's machine are the only places those levers earn their keep. Requiring the signing key wherever the app is hosted turns a configuration mistake into a build failure, and the staging project already has it. Checking the photo's address at the fetch covers every caller, including the next one.

## D-136 | 2026-09-17 | Advisories fail the build, and updates arrive weekly
- **Decision:** `pnpm audit --audit-level=high` runs in CI on every push, so a new high or critical advisory fails the build (M6-02, NFR-SEC-03). Where a dependency asks for a version with an advisory, `overrides` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` raises it: `browserslist` for the Serwist build, and `tmp` and `uuid` under Lighthouse CI. Where there is no fixed version, the advisory is listed in `auditConfig.ignoreGhsas` in the same file with the reason it cannot reach us and the date it was read: today that is `extract-zip`, twice, which reaches us only through Lighthouse CI's browser downloader, and the Lighthouse job measures with the runner's own Chrome. Updates arrive as weekly Dependabot pull requests, minor and patch grouped into one and a major version on its own, and CI decides whether they land. Licences are listed in `docs/SECURITY.md`: everything is permissive, with `mapbox-gl` the one dependency under commercial terms.
- **Options:** Leave `pnpm audit` to a person to remember; fail the build on every advisory including low; drop Lighthouse CI to be rid of its tree; pin every transitive version by hand.
- **Reason:** A check nobody runs is not a check, and a build that fails on a low advisory in a test tool teaches people to ignore it. Overrides move five advisories with one line each and no change to what the app serves, which the production build and the Lighthouse run both confirmed. An exception with the reason written next to it can be read and revisited; an exception in somebody's head cannot. Weekly grouped updates keep the reading small enough to actually do.

## D-137 | 2026-09-17 | One account books a hundred and twenty lessons an hour at most
- **Decision:** Booking now counts against the same limiter as invitations, learners added by hand and the waiting list: 120 an hour for one account, refused with `RATE_LIMITED` after that (M6-03, NFR-SEC-03). A weekly plan counts once, however many lessons it lays down, because it is one thing the person did. The job that extends open-ended plans books through its own function and is not counted. Supabase Auth counts sign-ins, codes, emails and texts itself, and the hosted numbers (30 sign-ins, 30 verifications, 30 emails and 10 texts an hour) are now held to by a test, so raising them to the local stack's numbers cannot pass unnoticed.
- **Options:** No limit on booking, since a slot can only be taken once; a much smaller limit, such as 20 an hour; counting each lesson of a weekly plan.
- **Reason:** A slot can be taken once, but an attempt costs a transaction, a free-times read and a hold on a card's worth of work, and nothing else stopped a script from making thousands. A school booking a day's lessons in a sitting is the busiest honest case and is nowhere near 120; a learner books a handful. Counting each lesson of a weekly plan would refuse an honest plan of 52 weeks at the second one.

## D-138 | 2026-09-17 | The content security policy is enforced, and keeps inline scripts
- **Decision:** The policy moves from report-only to enforced on every response (M6-04, NFR-SEC-03), and moves out of `next.config.ts` into `apps/web/src/lib/security-headers.ts`, where it can be read and tested on its own. It keeps `'unsafe-inline'` for scripts and styles, and allows `'unsafe-eval'` only outside a build. Every third-party origin is the set its provider publishes rather than the set we have watched work: Stripe's, including `*.js.stripe.com` and Link's `link.com`, and Mapbox's `api.mapbox.com` and `events.mapbox.com` with `worker-src blob:`. Origins that depend on the environment, the Supabase project and its socket, PostHog and Sentry, are read from it, and left out when it does not set them. Proof is in three places: an end to end spec that opens every public page and every portal screen for all four roles at both widths and fails on anything refused, unit tests that hold each provider's set to what it documents, and a watch on the real card form in the Stripe test-mode run.
- **Options:** Leave it report-only until a violation report service is wired up; use a nonce and drop `'unsafe-inline'`; allow only the origins seen in a local run; enforce it on the portals and leave the public pages report-only.
- **Reason:** A report-only policy with nowhere to send reports is a comment, not a control, and it had already sat that way for a milestone. A nonce is the stronger answer, and the one to reach for later, but a nonce has to be minted for each request, so every page would have to be rendered for each request: that is exactly what M5-24 spent its time undoing, and the public pages are the ones that must stay prerendered. Inline scripts are limited to what Next itself writes, and the rest of the policy, `object-src 'none'`, `base-uri 'self'`, `form-action 'self'` and `frame-ancestors 'none'`, still closes the ways an injected script is usually made useful. Watching what a local run happens to load would encode today's accident: Stripe says plainly that it starts frames on origins of its own choosing, and a checkout that fails only in production is the worst way to learn that. Link is admitted although the product owner has not chosen whether to offer it, because the Payment Element offers it unless it is switched off, and admitting it costs nothing.

## D-139 | 2026-09-17 | The load tests measure the API, against the local stack, and put back what they book
- **Decision:** The k6 tests (`load/reads.js`, `load/booking.js`) measure what NFR-PERF-02 is about: the calls the app makes, over HTTP, as a person's own session. The reads are the anonymous ones a learner's evening is made of, the write is `create_booking` with enough learners at once that two go for the same slot. A whole page is measured too, but under its own looser threshold, because a page's requirement is NFR-PERF-01, which Lighthouse holds (M5-24). The tests run against the local stack, never a hosted project, and the scripts refuse to start if pointed at one. `scripts/load-fixtures.mjs` builds what they need from the seeded database over the same API, including the days each instructor actually works. Bookings are made from three weeks out, past the seeded lessons and the days the end to end tests use, inside the eight weeks a Business takes bookings for, and `pnpm load:clean` cancels them afterwards. A learner told the slot has gone is a pass, not a failure. The job runs in CI on every push, with the timings in its annotations.
- **Options:** Load test the hosted staging project; drive the Server Actions rather than the RPCs; hold a page to the same 300 ms as an API call; leave the bookings in the diary; run it weekly on a schedule instead of on every push.
- **Reason:** Hammering staging would spend the product owner's Supabase allowance and teach us about a shared, throttled instance rather than about our own queries, which is where a missing index or an expensive policy shows. A Server Action is a POST with a header Next owns and may change, so a test built on that shape would break for reasons that have nothing to do with speed; the RPC underneath is the work, and the action adds only Next's own overhead, which Lighthouse already measures. Holding a rendered page to the API's 300 ms would fail the build for something the PRD does not ask, and hiding the page measurement entirely would leave nobody watching it. Bookings left behind would fill the diary until the next seed and slowly starve the test of slots, which is exactly what the first local run did. On every push while the app is small: a load test nobody looks at is the same as no load test, and this one takes about four minutes. *Amended 17 September 2026: the first run in CI booked for a fixed length of time and ran out of things to book. What a load run can book is finite twice over, by the free hours in a diary and by the 120 lessons an hour one account may book (D-137), and past either one it stops measuring bookings and starts measuring refusals: 266 bookings, then 21,347 rounds that found nothing free and 112 refusals. It now books a fixed 200, and a threshold on how many were really made fails the run if it degenerates again. A booking refused because the slot has gone answers 400, so counting non-2xx answers as failures would have counted the very race the test exists to create; what is held instead is that every answer is one of the three we mean.*

## D-140 | 2026-09-17 | Zod is told not to ask to compile a validator in the browser
- **Decision:** `packages/core/src/zod.ts` is the one module that imports Zod. It calls `z.config({ jitless: true })` in a browser before handing `z` out, and eslint's `no-restricted-imports` stops anything else importing `zod` directly. A schema is built as its own module is read, so anything that has `z` has already run the switch.
- **Options:** Allow `'unsafe-eval'` in a build; let the refusals happen, since Zod catches them; tell the end to end test to ignore this one refusal; call the switch once at the root of the app, from `instrumentation-client.ts`.
- **Reason:** Zod builds a faster validator with the Function constructor where it can, and finds out whether it can by trying `Function('')` as the first schema is built. The enforced policy (D-138) refuses that, Zod reads the schema as it goes instead, and nothing breaks; but the browser reports a refusal on every page that builds a schema. Allowing `'unsafe-eval'` to quiet it would give up the directive that stops an injected string from becoming code, for a speed we do not need on a form of six fields. Leaving the refusals would fill Sentry from M6-10 with a warning that means nothing, and a policy that cries wolf is one nobody reads. Teaching the test to ignore it would hide the next real one. On a server there is no such policy and the faster validator is worth having, so the switch is thrown in a browser only.
- **Also:** the first answer was a call in `instrumentation-client.ts`, which Next runs before the app is interactive. It worked, and it cost the public profile page seven points of Lighthouse: importing Zod at the root of the app put Zod in the bundle of every page, including the pages that had none, and the profile page went from 96 to 89 with its largest paint 2.6 seconds to 3.4. That is the score M5-24 spent a milestone winning, so it was undone. Routing the imports that already exist through one module moves no bytes at all.
- **Also:** this only showed up in CI, because the policy allows a script to run from a string while a person is developing and not in a build, so the end to end tests mean the most against a build. That is what CI runs them against, and it is now written next to the tests.

## D-141 | 2026-09-18 | The day the product runs on is a day in the United Kingdom
- **Decision:** `private.today()` returns `(now() at time zone 'Europe/London')::date`, and every function that compares a stored date with today uses it instead of `current_date`: the badge checks on the booking page, the profile pages, the open times and search, the verification rule, the two badge jobs, and the two age rules. A database test fails the build if any function in `public` or `private` goes back to `current_date`, in its body or as a default argument.
- **Options:** Leave it, since the two agree for 23 hours a day; set the database's own timezone to Europe/London; pass the day in from the app; make the end to end test use a date two days back so it cannot land in the hour.
- **Reason:** `current_date` is the database's day and the database keeps UTC. Through British Summer Time, between midnight in London and midnight in UTC, they are different days: for that hour a badge that ran out yesterday still counted as in date, an instructor stayed bookable when they should not have been, and a learner was a day younger than they are. The product is British and the app has always worked in Europe/London, so the database has been answering a different question from the one being asked. An end to end test found it at ten to one in the morning, and moving the test's dates would have hidden it rather than fixed it. Changing the database's timezone would fix it everywhere at once, but it would also change every other cast and comparison in a way nothing in the tests would notice; naming the rule in one function changes only what was wrong, and the test keeps it that way.

## D-142 | 2026-09-18 | Every screen is swept, and text at twice the size never pushes a page sideways
- **Decision:** `e2e/specs/accessibility-sweep.spec.ts` opens every public page and every portal screen, for all four roles at both widths, and holds three things: the WCAG 2.2 AA scan finds nothing serious or critical, the page needs no sideways scrolling with the text set to 200%, and the keyboard reaches every control, can get past the navigation in one press, and can see where it is. The screens live in one list (`e2e/support/screens.ts`) that the content security policy sweep (M6-04) reads too, so a new screen is watched by both from the day it exists.
- **Options:** Keep to the scans the flows already do; sample a few screens for the text size; check the keyboard by hand once and write it down.
- **Reason:** The flows scan what they visit, in the states a person puts it in, which catches more than a sweep; what they cannot catch is a screen no flow opens, and none of them ever resizes the text or uses the keyboard alone. Sampling would have found two of the fifteen places the text size broke a layout. What the sweep found: no serious contrast or naming faults anywhere, one secondary submit button greyed out and unreadable for the moment before a page came alive, no way past the navigation on any portal screen, and fifteen screens that had to be scrolled sideways at 200% text. All are fixed, and the fixes are mostly in the design system rather than in the screens: a button's label may wrap and the button grows to hold it, a status pill and a chip the same, a switch's words give way before its control, a tab bar's label is shortened, a long word breaks rather than leaving its box, a grid that only names its columns from a breakpoint up now names one below it as well, and rows that hold a time, a name and a price wrap when they cannot share a line.
- **Also:** the bar here, no sideways scrolling at 200% text on a 390 px screen, is stricter than WCAG 1.4.4, which asks only that nothing is lost. It is the bar that can be tested without a person looking, and holding it means the looser one holds too.

## D-143 | 2026-09-18 | The legal pages are drafts that say what the product does, and say that they are drafts
- **Decision:** Five pages at `/privacy`, `/cookies`, `/terms`, `/business-terms` and `/accessibility`, linked from the footer of every public page and listed in the sitemap. Each one says what the product actually does, in the product's own English, and leaves the wording only a solicitor may write in a marked block headed "For the solicitor". Until one has been through a page it carries a notice at the top saying it is a draft and is not in force, and the line under the title says it has not been checked. The data processing terms for a Business live inside the terms for instructors and schools rather than in a separate document. The accessibility statement is not a draft: it is ours to write, and it is written.
- **Options:** Wait for a solicitor and ship nothing; buy a template and fill it in; publish an unmarked draft; keep the processing terms in their own document.
- **Reason:** The facts in these pages are the part only we know: which company holds what, where it sits, how long it is kept, what the app asks a learner to tick before lessons start inside the 14 days. A solicitor charges for their judgement, not for discovering our architecture, so giving them a draft that is already right about the product is both cheaper and safer than a template that is right about nothing. An unmarked draft is the real risk: somebody would take it for the finished thing, so every page says plainly that it is not, and a test fails the build if a notice or a marked gap goes missing. The processing terms sit inside the Business terms because one agreement is what a Business signs; if the solicitor wants them separate, moving them is a page move.

## D-144 | 2026-09-18 | The cookie answer lives on the device, and nothing is loaded before it is yes
- **Decision:** The answer is kept in the browser's own storage, not in a cookie the server reads, and React reads it through `useSyncExternalStore` so the question and the counting always agree. The counting library is fetched by a dynamic import the first time somebody says yes: before that nothing is loaded, no request is made and nothing is stored. Saying no afterwards stops it and forgets what it kept. The question is asked once, sits over the page rather than pushing it down, and can be answered again from the cookies page. PostHog is started with no session recording, no autocapture, and a profile only for somebody who has signed in.
- **Options:** A cookie the server reads, so the page knows the answer before it renders; a banner rendered on the server; loading the library always and telling it to hold its tongue until consent.
- **Reason:** A cookie the server reads makes every page depend on the request, which is exactly what stops a page being prerendered, and the public pages must be (NFR-PERF-04, M5-24). Loading the library and asking it to wait still means a third party's code is on the page and a request has already gone out, which is not what "no analytics before consent" means. The dynamic import is the only version where a person who has not agreed is provably untouched, and the end to end test proves it by watching for a request that never comes.
- **Also:** while the tests run, the counting host points at a stand-in route on the app itself, so the proof needs no real service and nothing leaves the machine. Every test project starts from a browser that has already answered no, which is how most visitors arrive; only the spec about the question sees it asked.

## D-145 | 2026-09-18 | Writing .env.local keeps whatever is already set
- **Decision:** `pnpm db:env --force` now refreshes only the values that come from the local Supabase stack, keeps every other value that is already set, and copies the old file aside first.
- **Options:** Leave it, and remember not to pass `--force`; refuse `--force` when the file has anything in it.
- **Reason:** It used to rewrite the whole file from `.env.example`, which threw away every key somebody had pasted in by hand. It did exactly that during M6-08, and only the public values could be recovered, because a build has those baked in and never has the secret ones. A tool that can destroy something only its owner can replace should not be able to, whatever it is asked.

## D-146 | 2026-09-18 | The browser is told to upgrade http only where there is a certificate
- **Decision:** `upgrade-insecure-requests` is in the policy when the app is hosted, which is preview and production, rather than whenever the build is a production one (D-138, M6-08). `'unsafe-eval'` still goes by the build, as it did.
- **Options:** Leave it on every production build; leave it off everywhere and rely on every URL being https already.
- **Reason:** A production build runs on a laptop and in CI over http, and the directive upgraded the app's own requests to https, where nothing was listening; worse, the upgraded address no longer matched the origin the policy allows, so the policy refused its own request. That is what happened to the counting host in the end to end tests. Hosted is the condition the directive is actually about: there is a certificate, everything should be https, and anything that is not should be upgraded rather than sent in the clear.

## D-147 | 2026-09-18 | What the product counts is said on the page as well as sent
- **Decision:** `track` calls the counting service and also dispatches a `counted` event on the page carrying the same name and properties. The end to end tests read those, the unit tests next to `track` hold the call to the service, and the spec about the cookie question holds that nothing at all goes over the network before somebody says yes.
- **Options:** Assert on the network request the service makes; point the tests at the real service with a made up key; assert nothing and trust the wiring.
- **Reason:** Asserting on the service's own request means testing the service: PostHog batches, waits on its remote configuration and retries, and against a stand-in host it never flushed at all, which says nothing about whether we counted the right thing. What matters is ours: that the event has the right name and properties, that it is counted exactly once for a screen, and that a person who has not agreed is untouched. The dispatched event makes all three visible from outside without a test reaching inside a module, and it is the hook a debug panel would use. Feature flags are switched off in the init, because the product reads none, and asking for them on every page load both costs a request and holds the counting until the answer arrives.
- **Owner check:** with a real key on staging, confirm events arrive in PostHog. Nothing here can prove the service's own transport.

## D-148 | 2026-09-18 | Your own data comes as a file and a page you can print
- **Decision:** One function, `public.export_my_data()`, answers about whoever is signed in and nobody else, and writes every export to the audit trail as it goes (M6-11, NFR-PRV-03, NFR-SEC-06). Two ways to have it: `/account/data.json` downloads the file, and `/account/data` shows the same thing to read, with a button that prints it, which is how a person makes a PDF of it. A learner's export carries their lessons, what they paid and how they are getting on; an instructor's carries their profile, their hours and the lessons they taught, with no learner's name in them. A licence number is ciphertext the database cannot read, so the export says one is held rather than printing something useless, and a saved map point is left out because the postcode it came from is already there.
- **Options:** Generate a PDF with a layout library; email the export instead of handing it over; build the export in the app from a dozen queries.
- **Reason:** A printable page is how the product already gives somebody a receipt, every browser and phone turns one into a PDF in a keystroke, and it is the same page the person has just read rather than a second rendering that can disagree with it. A layout library would add a large dependency, its own fonts and its own bugs, for a document nobody reads twice. One function in the database means the answer is decided in the one place that can enforce who is asking, and the audit entry cannot be forgotten by a caller. Building it in the app would need a dozen queries under RLS, each a chance to leak somebody else's row or to miss one of the person's own.
- **Owner check:** if a solicitor wants a generated PDF rather than a printable page, it is a page to render rather than a data question, and the export function does not change.

## D-149 | 2026-09-18 | Deleting an account takes the person out of the records, and waits a week first
- **Decision:** A request waits seven days and can be called off in them (`cancel_account_deletion`), and is then carried out by a nightly job. What it does: lessons still to come are cancelled, the learner's part of every Business ends, notes written about them go, their date of birth and licence number go, their devices and notification choices go, an instructor's public profile loses its name, words, picture, badge number and booking link, their way in to every Business ends, and our own record of them keeps only 'Deleted account'. The account itself is banned for a thousand years, its password removed and its sessions deleted, which is the same mechanism a suspension uses (D-126) and refuses both a new sign in and any session already open. Payments, refunds, receipts and credit stay exactly as they are, because the law says a Business keeps them for six years, and none of them carries the person's name. Every deletion is written to the audit trail.
- **Options:** Delete the rows outright and let the cascades run; do it the moment it is asked for; keep the account able to sign in and merely hide it; refund paid lessons still to come.
- **Reason:** Deleting the rows would take the Business's financial records with them, which the law does not allow, and would break a receipt that has already been issued. Seven days is the gap between a decision made in temper and one meant: the screen says exactly when it happens and offers a way back until then. Banning the account rather than deleting it is what makes "cannot sign in" true for sessions already open as well as for new ones, and it is a path the product already trusts. Money owed for a lesson still to come is between the learner and their Business: the product cancels the lesson and says so on the screen, because a platform that refunds on somebody's behalf from an account that no longer exists would be making a decision that is not its to make.
- **Also:** the one function that erases an account is the single exception to the rule that no owner-privileged function may touch the private notes table (LRN-04). The rule stands, the exception is named in the test that enforces it, and two further tests hold that function to mentioning the table once, in a delete.

## D-150 | 2026-09-18 | Errors are reported, read before they are sent, and silent without a DSN
- **Decision:** `@sentry/nextjs` watches the browser, the server and the jobs, started in one place (`lib/errors/sentry.ts`) and doing nothing at all unless `NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN` is set. Every report goes through a scrubber of ours first: the query string, the cookies and the body are dropped, a header whose name says it is a secret is removed, only the account's id survives of the person it happened to, and anything attached under a telling name goes. No session recording, ever. The build is not wrapped, so no source maps are uploaded and the Sentry command line tool is never downloaded.
- **Options:** Wrap the build with `withSentryConfig` for source maps and a tunnel; write our own reporter against Sentry's HTTP protocol; keep Sentry only on the server.
- **Reason:** An error report is useful because it carries the state around a mistake, which is exactly why it must be read before it is sent: a query string can carry an invitation token, a cookie carries a session, and a form field can carry a password. The scrubber is a plain function, so seven tests hold it to that rather than trusting a setting. Not wrapping the build keeps the one file everything depends on untouched, which matters more than a readable stack trace until there is a DSN to send one to; uploading source maps is a line in the runbook for the day the product owner wants it. A reporter of our own would mean reimplementing breadcrumbs, grouping and releases, which is what the service is for.
- **Owner check:** the DSN, the uptime checks and where an alert goes are the product owner's to set. Until the DSN is set, nothing is reported and nothing is proved: the tests here hold what we send, not that it arrives.

## D-151 | 2026-09-18 | The pages are asked for once before Lighthouse measures them
- **Decision:** `pnpm lighthouse` asks for each measured page once before the run (`scripts/lighthouse-warm.mjs`), and the list of pages lives in `lighthouserc.cjs` so the warm up and the run cannot drift apart.
- **Options:** Leave it, and read the median of three; raise the number of runs until the cold one washes out; lower the threshold.
- **Reason:** The very first request to a just started server pays for rendering that page. No visitor after the first ever pays it, and a median of three that includes it is a median of something nobody experiences: in CI the first run came in 10 to 20 points below the other two and dragged the median to the edge of the threshold. Asking once first measures the page as it is served, which is the thing the requirement is about. Raising the number of runs would hide the cold one behind arithmetic while making every CI run longer, and lowering the threshold would be answering a measurement problem with a product concession.

## D-152 | 2026-09-18 | The cookie question is in the page from the start, and painted before React decides
- **Decision:** The question is rendered with the page, hidden by the stylesheet, and revealed by a fourteen line script in the head that reads the saved answer before the first paint. Only its two buttons are a client component. Saying yes or no removes the attribute, which hides it at once.
- **Options:** Leave React to decide after hydration, as it did; read the answer from a cookie the server can see; make the question smaller so it is not the largest thing on the page.
- **Reason:** Deciding in React meant the question appeared about three seconds in, and Lighthouse then measured *it* as the largest contentful paint on every public page: the element it named was this paragraph, with 86% of the time spent waiting for the render rather than the bytes. The pages fell from 95 to between 85 and 88, under the 90 the requirement asks for. Painted with the page it is either in the first frame or never painted at all, and the scores went back to 95 and 96 with the largest paint at 2.7 seconds. A cookie the server reads would make every page dynamic, which is the thing D-144 exists to avoid. Making the question smaller would be tuning a number rather than fixing what it measures.
- **Also:** hidden by default, so a page whose script never runs never shows it. Without a script nothing can be counted either, so there is nothing to ask about.

## D-153 | 2026-09-18 | The app asks for the Stripe values Phase 1 uses, and live keys stay in production
- **Decision:** With Stripe on, the app requires the publishable key, the secret key and the Connect webhook secret, and no longer the platform webhook secret. A live key (`sk_live_`, `rk_live_`, `pk_live_`) is refused anywhere but `APP_ENV=production`, and a test key is refused there, whichever payments provider is selected. The Stripe values go in Vercel for Production only (RUNBOOK 3.7 step 9); Supabase needs none.
- **Options:** Keep requiring the platform secret and create a second endpoint in Stripe only to have one to paste; check only that the two keys agree with each other; leave the keys unchecked, as before.
- **Reason:** Platform events come only from Stripe Billing, which arrives with ADM-10 in Phase 2 (D-022). The runbook said to leave that secret empty while the app refused to start without it, so the first production deploy would have stopped at boot. The publishable key was optional, so a production without it would have shown every learner "Card payments are not set up here yet" instead of failing the deploy. And on 18 September a live secret key sat next to a test publishable key in `.env.local`: nothing used it, because payments were on the fake, but the same mix in Vercel would either take real money for demo data or refuse every card. Checking the mode costs one prefix and names the key at fault. The account behind each key is not checked, because the part of a key that carries it is not a format Stripe documents.

## D-154 | 2026-09-18 | Supabase stays free until the first real user, then this project becomes production | Product owner
- **Decision (product owner):** Keep the organisation and the project on the free plan until the first real user. Then upgrade this same organisation and project to Pro and make it production, rather than creating a separate production project now.
- **Options:** A separate production project in its own Pro organisation now (about $25 a month), with staging kept free; Pro with point in time recovery from the start (about $100 a month more).
- **Reason:** No real data exists yet, so there is nothing a backup would protect, and paying before there is a user buys nothing.
- **What it means:** Until the upgrade there are no backups at all and no leaked password check, and the restore drill (M6-14) waits for it. Before the first real user: upgrade to Pro; remove the demo data, because seeded instructors, learners and anything that looks like a review must never reach production (rule 7), most simply by resetting the hosted database and applying the migrations, since nothing real is in it; set `APP_ENV=production` and the production values in Vercel (RUNBOOK 3.7 step 9); turn on the leaked password check; then run the drill. Staging then needs a new home when one is wanted: a second, free project.

## D-155 | 2026-09-18 | Text codes on staging, and the CLI setting that reads backwards
- **Decision:** The staging config now declares text codes: Twilio, no sign up with a phone number alone, one code a minute to a number. In `[auth.sms]` it says `enable_confirmations = false`, which is what keeps a changed mobile needing a code on a hosted project. `scripts/supabase.mjs` reads the hosted setting back after every push and fails the push if confirmations are off.
- **Options:** Keep the text settings in the dashboard and out of the file, as before; declare `true`, to match the local file and the setting's name.
- **Reason:** Pushing `true`, as the local file says, switched "Enable phone confirmations" off on the staging project (`sms_autoconfirm` became true), so a changed mobile would have counted as verified without a code (AUTH-02), while the CLI's diff reported the two as matching. It was caught on the dashboard within minutes and pushed back. The local stack reads the same key the other way round: the verify phone end to end test enters a code and passes. A file that says `false` to mean on is a trap, so the comment beside it says so, and the check after the push means a future CLI that fixes the inversion fails the push instead of quietly turning the code off.

## D-156 | 2026-09-19 | pgTAP takes today from the UK, and runs a second time on another day
- **Decision:** Every pgTAP test that means today now says `private.today()`, the day the functions under test have used since M6-06a, and lesson times are UK wall-clock times rather than midnight in the session's time zone plus some hours. CI runs the suite a second time with the database clock in a time zone whose date is not the UK's (`pnpm db:test:another-day`), so a test that mixes the two fails on every run.
- **Options:** Schedule CI away from the hour after midnight; retry a failed database job; forbid `current_date` in test files with a text check.
- **Reason:** At 00:06 UK time on 19 September the database job failed: it was still the 18th in UTC, the tests took today from the session and the functions took it from the UK, so a learner a day short of sixteen was let through and a badge expiring today was already out of date. It could only happen in one hour of twenty-four, which is how it outlived M6-06a. A retry would hide a real difference, and a text check would catch `current_date` but not the next way of reading the session's clock. Running on another day catches all of them, and costs fifteen seconds.

## D-157 | 2026-09-19 | Browser error reports go through our own server
- **Decision:** The browser library sends its reports to `/api/reports`, which passes on reports for our own Sentry project only, with none of the visitor's headers. The content security policy no longer admits Sentry. Server reports still go straight to Sentry, from Vercel. When Sentry turns a report away, the route logs Sentry's status and its short reason, never the report. Product owner's choice, 19 September, before the beta.
- **Options:** Keep sending from the browser and say in the privacy notice that Sentry stores an approximate town; the tunnel built into `withSentryConfig`, which means wrapping the build (D-150 set that aside).
- **Reason:** With "Prevent storing of IP addresses" on and the scrubber in front (D-150), a report from a browser still arrived with the visitor's town, worked out by Sentry from their address before it dropped the address: the one thing the scrubber cannot remove, because it is not in the report. Through our own server, the only address Sentry sees is Vercel's. The route refuses other projects, other sites and anything larger than an error report, so it is no more use to anybody else than the public DSN already was. It also gets reports past blockers that stop requests to Sentry by name.

## D-158 | 2026-09-19 | Nightly housekeeping keeps what the privacy notice promises, and no more
- **Decision:** Every night the maintenance sweep removes stored pictures that nothing in the database points to any more and that are more than a day old, and prunes audit rows more than two years old. A verification decision stops pointing to the badge picture, and erasing an account now stops pointing to the badge as well as the photo. `system_unreferenced_files` lists the pictures, only inside the folders the product writes; the job removes them through the storage API with the secret key. `system_prune_audit_log` is the one way rows leave the append-only audit table: the guard lets a row go only while that function has marked the transaction, and only if it is more than two years old.
- **Options:** A queue of files to remove, filled by each place that stops using one; removing a picture at the moment it stops being used, inside the same request; leaving the pictures and changing the privacy notice instead.
- **Reason:** Writing the legal review pack on 19 September found three promises in the privacy notice that nothing kept: the audit trail was kept forever, a badge picture stayed after its check, and a deleted person's photos stayed in storage, a profile photo at a public address. One rule, that a picture nothing points to goes, covers all three and the photos people replace, and it heals what happened before it existed; a queue would have to be filled correctly by every present and future caller. The day's grace is for a picture uploaded and not yet saved to a profile. Storage refuses deletion from SQL, so removing a file needs the storage API, and so the secret key: that is the exception to the rule that the key only calls `system_*` RPCs, as narrow as D-068's, since the job removes only what such an RPC has listed.

## D-159 | 2026-09-19 | The notification bell: top right on a phone, beside the name at the top of the menu on a larger screen
- **Decision:** Every learner, instructor and school screen has a bell that opens the notifications (NTF-01), with a red count of the unread ones. On a phone it is at the top right of a new bar across the top of the screen, with the name on its left; the bar scrolls with the screen, and is hidden from the md breakpoint, where the bell sits beside the name at the top of the sidebar. The admin portal, which is for larger screens only, has it in the sidebar too. The badge says up to 9 and then "9+", and a screen reader hears the exact number ("Notifications, 12 unread"). The bell is drawn at once and the count streams in behind it (D-029), so no screen waits for it, and the count is asked for once per request however many places show it. The Notifications row stays in More and Account. The phone's position is the product owner's choice; the larger screen's is mine.
- **Options:** On a larger screen, the top right of each screen's header, as on a phone, or an entry in the sidebar's list with the count beside it. On a phone, a tab in the bottom bar, or a top bar that stays put while the screen scrolls.
- **Reason:** Top right is where people look for notifications on a phone, and where the product owner asked for it. A larger screen here has no bar across the top: each screen's header carries its own title and, at its top right, its own actions (a new lesson, adding a learner), so a bell there would crowd them and move about from screen to screen. The sidebar is the one thing on every screen, and its top is where Linear, Notion and Slack keep theirs; beside the name it is in the same place everywhere and one click away, and the sidebar's list keeps to PRD 8.2. The tab bar is set by PRD 8.2 and already holds four or five tabs. A bar that stays put would take 56 px from every screen on a phone that already gives 64 px to the tab bar, and the diary is a long scroll. The badge stops at "9+" so it stays the size of a dot; the list says how many.

## D-160 | 2026-09-19 | Installing the app is always in the menu, and the school Overview offers it
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for both on 19 September; the page and its details are mine. Every More and Account menu ends with an Install the app row, which opens `/account/install`. That page answers for the device it is on: the browser's own prompt where there is one; Safari's three taps on an iPhone or iPad, saying that a newer iPhone keeps Share behind the three dots; the way to Safari from any other browser there; and otherwise the browser's menu, which is where installing lives before a browser offers it. Unlike the card, it stays after somebody says not now, since they went looking for it, and not now no longer throws the browser's prompt away, so the page can still use it. Inside the installed app the row is left out and the page says the app is there. The school Overview now has the card that Today and Home have had since M4-08.
- **Options:** A sheet from the menu rather than a page; the card on every screen, or never going away; leaving the row out with a CSS display-mode query.
- **Reason:** The product owner could not find a way to install on a phone. The card was on two screens, not on a school's, and one not now took it away for good on that device. A menu is where people look for what they skipped, and a page gives the steps room and a link of their own, as every other row in those menus has. The row is left out by the check the card already uses, the display mode or Safari's own flag, so there is one check and the tests can pretend to be the installed app; Chromium cannot emulate the display mode, so a CSS query could not be tested. Inside the app the row is taken out as the page comes alive only when the menu is the first screen opened; reached from a tab, it is never drawn. An iPhone shows notifications only from an app on its home screen (NTF-01), so the iPhone steps say so.

## D-161 | 2026-09-19 | The beta runs on Supabase's free plan until 20 people or the first card payment | Product owner
- **Decision (product owner):** Stay on the free plan into the beta, and upgrade this same project to Pro once 20 beta users are on it or card payments start, whichever comes first. This amends D-154, which upgraded at the first real user. What the free plan lacks is covered meanwhile. `pnpm db:backup` makes an encrypted backup of the hosted database, to be run weekly and before every database change and kept for five weeks; the privacy notice now says so. And the shortest password is 10 characters rather than 8, in the app and in Supabase Auth, while there is no check against leaked passwords.
- **Options:** Pro now, about $25 a month, as D-154 had it; the free plan with nothing in place of backups.
- **Reason:** The product owner wants to test the market with a small beta before paying. Checked what the free plan lacks for about 20 people. No backups at all, and no leaked password check: both covered as above. Pausing after a week without activity: it does not happen while the job runner reaches the database every five minutes. 500 MB of database, 1 GB of files and 5 GB of traffic a month: 20 people use a fraction. One day of logs and no support: Sentry already watches the app. The backups are ours, encrypted with a passphrase kept off the machine, and a drill restored one into an empty stack with every table matching. The minimum stays a length, not a mix of characters (NCSC). A password chosen under the old rule still signs in; new passwords and changed ones need ten.

## D-162 | 2026-09-19 | Lighthouse takes the median of five runs
- **Decision:** CI runs Lighthouse five times a page, not three, and still fails a page whose median is under 90 (NFR-PERF-04).
- **Options:** Three runs, re-running CI when the profile page lands on the line; lowering the bar; a faster profile page.
- **Reason:** On the same code the profile page's runs go from the mid seventies to 96 (a cold first run is usually the low one), so a median of three sat on the line: 90 on 19 September before the bell, 90 after it, and 88 on `main` the same afternoon, with nothing on the page changed. Locally it scores 95, with the cookie question as its largest paint, as D-152 intends. Five is what Lighthouse advises. The bar does not move, and a page that is really slower still fails.

## D-163 | 2026-09-19 | Staff who are more than staff carry on from a phone
- **Decision:** On a phone the admin portal still asks for a larger screen (PRD 8.2), and now also offers the person's other portals: Open your school, Open your diary, Open your lessons, in the order they would land in.
- **Options:** Land staff on another portal when they are on a phone; leave it.
- **Reason:** Staff land on admin first, so the product owner's own account, a school's owner made super admin, would have met a dead end on the phone at every sign in. Choosing a portal by screen size on the server means guessing the screen; offering the way on is exact.

## D-164 | 2026-09-19 | Only the instructor moves a lesson; a learner asks them | Product owner
- **Decision (product owner):** A learner no longer moves a lesson themselves. Move on their lesson says only their instructor can move it, and to contact them directly to ask. The instructor, and anybody who manages the Business's bookings, still moves a lesson at any time, and the learner is told. Cancelling is unchanged. This amends BOK-08, which let a learner move their own lesson outside the cancellation window.
- **Options:** Keep BOK-08 as written; a move request the instructor accepts or declines in the app.
- **Reason:** The product owner wants every change to an instructor's diary to go through the instructor. The database refuses a learner's move, not only the screen, so nothing but the instructor or the Business can do it. A request flow would be new work for something a phone call settles, and can follow if instructors ask. The learners' landing page no longer says a learner can move a lesson.

## D-165 | 2026-09-19 | Adding a learner from the booking sheet
- **Decision:** "Who is it for?" ends with "Add a new learner", which opens the Learners screen with its Add a learner sheet already open (`?add=1`). Closing that sheet takes the flag out of the address. The product owner asked for it.
- **Options:** A link beside the list; adding the learner inside the booking sheet itself.
- **Reason:** The list is where an instructor looks when the learner is missing. It stays the phone's own select, and its last choice says what it does, so the change of screen is not a surprise (WCAG 3.2.2). A form inside the booking form would be a second form in a sheet; the Learners sheet already has both ways of adding somebody.

## D-166 | 2026-09-19 | A lesson opens into one sheet, with the learner's number and a reminder sent by hand
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. Tapping a lesson on Today, or in the diary's day or week view, opens one sheet. It has when and what the lesson is, where the money stands, the pickup point with directions, and Call, Text and WhatsApp when the learner has a mobile. Then "Send a reminder" by email or by SMS; Mark paid, Move and Cancel through the diary's own sheets; and a way to the learner's card. The details are read when the sheet opens. A reminder sent by hand goes through `request_lesson_reminder`, which checks the caller teaches the lesson or runs the Business's bookings, allows one of each an hour for a lesson, refuses a text on a plan without texts, writes the audit trail and asks the job runner. The job sends it the way the automatic reminders go: their words, the learner's own settings, and a text only on a plan with texts, to somebody with a number. The sheet learns whether a text is possible from `lesson_reminder_options`, which answers yes or no and never names the plan, since billing is the owner's (D-123).
- **Options:** The contact buttons on every card; sending the reminder straight from the server; reading the plan to decide the SMS button; a WhatsApp message with the lesson written in.
- **Reason:** The card is where an instructor already looks, and one sheet keeps every card small on a phone. The learner's number is already on their card for the instructor (LRN-02); reading it when the sheet opens keeps it out of every list and every copy a phone keeps for no signal. Sending through the reminder job means one set of words, and a learner who switched email reminders off is not emailed because somebody pressed a button. WhatsApp opens a chat with the number and nothing more: the instructor writes their own message, as they do today. The cards stay openable only in the instructor's own diary and Today, since the sheet reads through the instructor portal.

## D-167 | 2026-09-19 | Upcoming lessons under Today's
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. Under Today's lessons, "Upcoming lessons" lists the next five lessons after today that are on, each opening its sheet (D-166), with "Show more" adding five at a time up to fifty. Requests still waiting for an answer, and lessons called off, are left out.
- **Options:** A link to the diary's week instead; loading more without changing the address.
- **Reason:** What comes next is the question after what is on today, and five is a phone screen. More are asked for in the address, so it works before the page has come alive and a refresh keeps the place; past fifty, the diary is the better way to read ahead.

## D-168 | 2026-09-19 | Pickup points kept from the learner's card, and lessons start from the default
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. The learner's card lists their pickup points. The instructor adds one, corrects or removes the ones their Business added, and ticks the one where lessons start; the learner's own show as theirs and cannot be changed from the card, as the policies already had it. A lesson the instructor books now starts from the learner's default pickup point unless another is chosen, so it shows on Today, in the lesson's sheet and in Navigate. Making a pickup point the default now also takes the default off the learner's own: the trigger that does that runs as its owner, where it ran as the instructor, could not touch the learner's own, and so let the one-default rule refuse the change outright.
- **Options:** Letting the instructor rewrite the learner's own addresses; a pickup chooser in the booking sheet; lessons with no pickup unless the learner books them.
- **Reason:** A learner's own addresses are theirs (COV-04, and the policies since M0). The instructor adds a better one rather than rewriting theirs, and either of them can say which is where lessons start. The default is what an instructor would choose nine times in ten, and without it the pickup the product owner sets on the card would never reach a lesson: every lesson an instructor booked had none. A chooser for a single lesson can follow if instructors need one. The trigger was found by the pgTAP written for this, the first test to make a Business's pickup the default while the learner had their own.

## D-169 | 2026-09-19 | An instructor sets a skill on the learner's map by hand
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. On a learner's progress page the instructor taps any area of the skill map and sets where the learner is with it, 1 to 5, without writing a lesson record. Each rating set this way is kept as a row of its own in `skill_assessments`, with who set it, when and for which Business, and is written only through `rate_skill`. That allows the instructor who teaches the learner at the Business, or the Business's owner or manager, and nobody else, the learner included. For each area the map takes the latest rating, whether it came from a lesson record or was set by hand, and counts both. The learner sees it on their own map, and it is in their data export.
- **Options:** A lesson record holding only that rating; changing a lesson record's rating in place; a map that only lesson records can move.
- **Reason:** PRG-02 describes the map and PRG-01 how lessons move it. A rating set on the map is neither a lesson nor a record of one, so it gets a table of its own rather than an empty record in the learner's timeline. Keeping every rating, rather than overwriting one, keeps the history the map is made of, and the latest wins, as it already did among lesson records. The learner owns their progress (PRG-04), so what is set on it is theirs to see and to export.

## D-170 | 2026-09-19 | A learner's lessons to come on a month calendar
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. The learner's Lessons page has two tabs over the lessons to come: "Coming up", the list as before, and "Calendar", a month with a dot on each day that has a lesson. The calendar opens on the next lesson's day, in its month, even when that is next month. Tapping a day lists its lessons under the month, drawn as the list draws them, so one can be paid for or cancelled there too, and Move says to ask the instructor (D-164). A month turned to opens on its first lesson. Days before today cannot be chosen: what has happened stays under "Before now", below both tabs.
- **Options:** Each lesson written into its day on the calendar; the calendar as the only view; opening on today.
- **Reason:** A phone has room for a month of days but not for lessons written into them, so a dot marks the day and its lessons go underneath, as calendars on phones do. The list stays first because it answers "when is my next lesson" at a glance. Opening on the next lesson means the calendar never opens on an empty month while a lesson is booked. PRD 8.2 gives the learner a Lessons screen without saying how it looks, so this is a second way to see it rather than a feature beside it.

## D-171 | 2026-09-20 | The dashboard adds up over the days staff choose
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. The admin dashboard has a button each for today, this week, this month, the last 30 days and this tax year (6 April to 5 April), and two date fields for any other stretch of days. The last 30 days is what it shows unasked, as before. The range lives in the address (`?range=week`, or `?from=` and `?to=`), so a view can be kept, shared or opened again. `platform_dashboard` now takes the range as a pair of instants instead of working out 30 days from now, and refuses a range that runs backwards or one longer than five years. Figures about now rather than a stretch of days, such as Businesses in good standing, badges waiting and disputes open, are the same whatever the range.
- **Options:** A drop-down of ranges; the fixed 30 days with a second screen for history; ranges worked out in the browser.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for buttons rather than a menu, so every choice is one tap and the chosen one is visible. Days are London days turned into instants at the edges, the way the money dashboard's periods already work (MNY-01), so "today" means today in Leeds and the tax year starts on 6 April wherever the server is. Keeping the range in the address means the back button and a pasted link both work. Five years is longer than the platform has existed and stops one call reading the whole history.

## D-172 | 2026-09-20 | Five lists under the dashboard, folded away
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for them. Under the figures the dashboard carries five lists, each folded away until it is opened: the top ten earning schools, the top ten earning instructors of one, the schools teaching the most learners, the instructors of one teaching the most learners, and who is new to the platform, five at a time with "Show more". They cover the same days as the figures above them. Earning means what a Business took in those days, counted as the dashboard counts GMV: payments settled in the range, credit left out because it was counted when the package was bought. Most learners means learners with a lesson in the range that was not called off, counted once each however many lessons they had. New to the platform means Businesses made in the range, newest first. Every row is a link into the Businesses screen, searched by name. One database call brings all five.
- **Options:** A list per screen; the platform's own income as the ranking; loading each list when it is unfolded.
