- **Reason:** The product owner chose what the lists rank: what schools and instructors took, not what the platform earned from them, which is the Payments screen's job. Folded cards keep the dashboard a screen of figures, as asked, and `details` does that in plain HTML, so it works before the page comes alive and a screen reader is told whether a card is open. One call for all five is cheaper than five calls when each is unfolded, and the lists are small. The arrivals go five at a time from the address, the way Today's upcoming lessons do (D-167).

## D-173 | 2026-09-20 | The audit log comes away as a file, and that is written down
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. "Download as JSON" on the audit log hands over every entry the filters match, over the days chosen, newest first, up to five thousand, as a file named after the product, the days and the day it was taken. The file says what was asked for, how many entries it holds, and whether there were more than fit. Taking a copy is written to the log itself as `audit_log.exported`, with what was asked for and how many entries went into the file; reading a page on screen is still not recorded (D-130). The reading function's own guard went from a hundred rows to ten thousand, so one call fills a file rather than a hundred round trips, and the screen can still ask for one more than it shows.
- **Options:** CSV as well as JSON; a background job that emails a large file; recording nothing.
- **Reason:** JSON keeps what changed exactly as it was recorded, where CSV would flatten the before and after into text. A cap stops one request reading the whole history, and five thousand entries is more than a month of this platform; the file says when it was cut short, so staff narrow the days and take another. Reading a page is routine work, but carrying the log out of the platform is exactly the sensitive act NFR-SEC-06 means, so it leaves a trace, and the file's own row says what was taken.

## D-174 | 2026-09-20 | Payments is what the platform earned; Bookings is gone
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for both. The admin Payments screen, until now a placeholder (D-133), shows what schools and instructors have paid the platform over the days chosen: the fees kept from card payments, what those payments moved so the share can be read, who paid them with the plan they are on, and how many Businesses are on each plan now. Plan subscriptions are not charged for yet, so plan payments show as nothing and the screen says why (ADM-10, D-161). The admin Bookings screen is gone from the menu and the code: lessons are looked into through the learner or instructor, and viewing as them. `/admin/bookings` is now a page that is not found.
- **Options:** Leaving Bookings as a placeholder; showing lessons sold on the Payments screen; waiting for plan billing before building the screen.
- **Reason:** The product owner wants Payments to answer "what has the platform earned", which is a different question from the dashboard's GMV, and a screen that shows the fees now will show subscriptions when billing arrives. A Business's plan cannot be read from the table by staff (D-123), so the function reads it for them, having checked who is asking. Bookings had nothing to show that the learner's and instructor's own screens do not show better, and an empty menu entry is worse than none. The placeholder route for the admin portal went with it, since nothing else used it.

## D-175 | 2026-09-20 | A screen for who has asked to leave, and a reason asked for but not required
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. "Leaving" in the admin menu lists everybody with a deletion request waiting: who they are, what they said, the Business they belong to, how to reach them by email, phone or WhatsApp, and how long before the account is erased (seven days, D-149). A super admin who has put the trouble right calls the request off, giving a note that goes into the audit log with it; support staff read the list but call nothing off, as with suspending a Business (ADM-02). The person themselves can still call it off from their own account. The reason is asked for on the way out, as it already was, and stays optional: the field now says they need not answer and that telling us may let us put it right first.
- **Options:** Requiring a reason before the request goes through; letting any staff call a request off; a nightly email instead of a screen.
- **Reason:** The product owner chose to keep the reason optional. UK GDPR treats erasure as a right, and making somebody explain themselves before they may exercise it obstructs it; asking plainly, and saying why we ask, gets an answer often enough without standing in the way. Calling somebody's deletion off changes their account against what they asked for, so it belongs with the super admin's other powers and leaves a note saying who did it and why. Seven days is the whole window staff have, so the screen counts it down rather than making them work it out.

## D-176 | 2026-09-20 | Everybody on the platform has a number of their own
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. Every account carries a number, written as D and six digits: D000001 for the first, counting up, and past a million it grows rather than starting again. D000000 belongs to nobody, because the counter starts at one. A number is never given to anybody else, so somebody who deletes their account and signs up again is a new number; a sequence does that by never going backwards. Accounts already on the platform were numbered by when they joined, with the platform's super admin first, so the founder is D000001. Everybody sees their own ID on their account screen, and platform staff see it on every person's row and card, and can find somebody by it, typed as D000123, d123 or 123.
- **Options:** Reusing the row's UUID; a number per role rather than per account; keeping the ID for staff only.
- **Reason:** A UUID is unreadable over the phone, which is the point of an ID somebody quotes to support. One counter for every account, rather than one per role, means an ID names a person and nothing else. The product owner chose to show it to everybody: it is not secret, it reveals nothing beyond roughly when somebody joined, and support goes faster when the person can read it out. Never reusing a number keeps the audit log honest: an old entry about D000042 can never be read as being about somebody new.

## D-177 | 2026-09-20 | How an instructor's week is going, on Today
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. Under Today's lessons and the upcoming ones, an instructor sees how the week or the month so far is going: what they earned, drawn as a bar for each day and split by how each lesson was paid for (card, cash, bank or credit), with the hours booked and the learners taught beside it. This week runs Monday to Sunday, as the money dashboard's week does (MNY-01); this month runs from the first up to today, which is what "so far" means. Earnings count each lesson that is on and paid for, on the day it is taught, which is why credit is among the colours: no money arrives that day, but the lesson was paid for. The hours count every lesson that is on, paid for or not, and each learner is counted once. It is the instructor's own lessons, whichever Business they teach for.
- **Options:** Counting payments as they settle, the way the money dashboard does; a line rather than bars; putting the stats above Today's lessons.
- **Reason:** An instructor reads their day in lessons, so earnings by the day a lesson was taught is the figure they recognise; money settling later belongs on the Money screen, which still counts it that way. Bars a day wide show a week at a glance on a phone, and each bar carries its figures as words, so a screen reader reads the numbers rather than guessing at heights. Today's lessons stay at the top because that is what the app is opened for; how the week is going sits under them.

## D-178 | 2026-09-20 | Start lesson belongs to the lesson, and only when it is close
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. "Start lesson" was one button under the day, for whichever lesson came next, however far off it was. It now sits on the card of the lesson it starts, beside Navigate and Write record, and appears only from a quarter of an hour before the lesson until it ends, or whenever a lesson is already under way. Earlier in the day no lesson offers to be started.
- **Options:** Keeping the button under the day and only hiding it when nothing is close; an hour's window; showing it all day but asking "start early?" first.
- **Reason:** A button under the day says "start a lesson" without saying which, which is wrong when the day holds five. On its own card there is no doubt. A quarter of an hour is about when an instructor arrives at the pickup point, which is when starting it is the thing they want; before that the button is a way to start the wrong lesson by mistake, and a lesson started by mistake writes a record against the wrong hour.

## D-179 | 2026-09-20 | A lesson length the instructor sets, priced at the hourly rate
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. The booking sheet's "How long?" list ends with "A length of your own", which opens hours (one to four) and minutes (nought or thirty), so an instructor can book anything from an hour to four and a half on the half hour. It costs the hourly rate for the time it takes, to the penny: the instructor's own rate for that lesson type where they have one, otherwise the Business's. The price is still worked out by the database, never sent by the screen (R-05); the screen shows the same sum so nothing changes when the booking lands. A Business with no hourly price for a lesson type sells no length of its own for it, and the choice does not appear. The catalogue's own lengths keep the prices the catalogue gives them, which may be less than the hourly rate for a long lesson.
- **Options:** Letting the instructor type a price; adding every half hour to the catalogue; a free-text number of minutes.
- **Reason:** BOK-03 already says lesson durations are "60, 90, 120 minutes plus custom set by the Business", so a length outside the catalogue needs a price the Business owns rather than one typed at booking time, which is why the hourly rate does it. Two pickers rather than a free number keeps the length to something an instructor can teach and a diary can lay out. Four and a half hours is the longest anybody sat in a car for one lesson, and the shortest is an hour, which is what the rate is quoted in.

## D-180 | 2026-09-20 | A learner may tell us about a disability, and chooses who that helps
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. A learner is asked, at sign-up and on About you in their account, whether they have a disability, condition or learning difficulty, and what would help. Answering is their choice: the question opens on "Rather not say", nothing is required, and they may change it or take it off their record at any time. Saying yes asks what would help, because a yes on its own tells an instructor nothing. It is kept in a table of its own, not with the rest of their profile, and read by the learner, the instructor who teaches them, the people who run the Business they learn with, and platform staff, which is the same audience as the rest of their card (D-131). Nobody else, including other instructors at the same school. It is in their own copy of their data (NFR-PRV-03), goes with the account when it is deleted, and the privacy page says all of this in the same words.
- **Options:** Keeping it with the rest of the learner's profile; requiring an answer before sign-up finishes; showing it to the instructor only.
- **Reason:** Health is special category data under UK GDPR: it needs a clear purpose, a real choice, and the narrowest access that still serves the purpose. Asking at sign-up and leaving it answerable later means nobody is made to disclose a disability to finish signing up, and the wording says why we ask and who reads it before they answer. The product owner chose the card's audience, so a manager reassigning a learner sees what an instructor would; a table of its own keeps the reach of that choice explicit in the policies rather than buried in a column grant.

## D-181 | 2026-09-20 | The diary's buttons hold their place, and Today leads with how it is going
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for both. In the diary, "Back to today" no longer sits beside the arrows: it has a line of its own below them, so the arrows and the Day, Week and Month buttons stay exactly where they are whether or not it is on screen. Moving through the diary used to widen that row and push the views onto a second line, which moved the buttons under the reader's thumb. On Today, "How it is going" now comes first, above the day's lessons, which amends D-177, where it sat under them.
- **Options:** Keeping the row as it was and holding a gap for "Back to today"; putting it above the arrows; leaving the stats where they were.
- **Reason:** A control that moves when you press another one is worse than a control lower down the screen, and a row that reflows is exactly that. A line of its own costs one row of height and nothing else. As for the stats, the product owner reads the week's figures first and the day's lessons after, so Today opens that way round; the lessons are still the first thing under the setup card.

## D-182 | 2026-09-20 | A learner keeps their own pickup points, found from the postcode
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for both halves. A learner now has Pickup points on their Account tab, where they add, change and remove places of their own and tick the one lessons start from. A place added by their school stays the school's: they see it, and it is not theirs to rewrite. Adding one starts with the postcode, which is looked up as it is typed, names the place and drops a pin; the house number and street are typed under it, and the pin may be dragged to the door. Where a lookup service knows the addresses inside a postcode, the same form offers them as a list instead of the typed field, and picking one places the pin. The instructor's copy of the card is the same form, so both sides fill a pickup point in the same way.
- **Options:** A paid address service now (Ideal Postcodes, getAddress.io, Royal Mail PAF); a free-text address with no postcode lookup; a map the learner drops a pin on with no address at all.
- **Reason:** COV-04 wants the pickup point to be somewhere an instructor can actually stop, and a typed address alone is often not. postcodes.io is free, already behind `GeoProvider` with a cache table, and gets us the district, the point on the map and the validity of the postcode, which is most of the value; the house number is the only part it cannot give. The product owner chose to start free and pay later, so `GeoProvider` gained an optional `addresses(postcode)` and the form uses the list when a provider offers it, which is the whole change needed to buy a paid service later. The pin is optional and saved only when it is near the postcode it belongs to, so a stale pin from an earlier postcode can never send an instructor to the wrong town. A learner's own row carries no Business, which is what lets the policies tell "theirs" from "the school's" without a new column.

## D-183 | 2026-09-20 | A learner is asked a few things to start with, and may skip any of them
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. Once a learner has signed up, their home screen asks, one question at a time: where their lessons start, a disability, which gearbox, whether they take medication that could affect their driving, and whether they have passed the theory test within the last two years. Every question has a Skip beside it, a skipped question is never asked again, and every answer is on About you in their account afterwards, so skipping loses nothing. A question that is already answered is never asked: a learner who chose a gearbox at sign-up is asked the other four. Medication is health data, so it sits with the disability answer in `learner_health` and is read by exactly the same people (D-180); the gearbox, the theory pass and the list of skipped questions are ordinary profile data. The theory question asks about the last two years rather than storing a date, because that is how long a pass lasts. Their instructor and the people who run their Business read all of it on the learner's card.
- **Options:** A separate onboarding screen the learner has to finish before reaching the app; a checklist showing all five at once; making the questions required; a date for the theory pass.
- **Reason:** LRN-02 puts these details on the learner's profile, and the product owner wanted them asked rather than waited for. A wall in front of the app would cost us learners at the one moment they are keenest, and health questions must never be a condition of using the service, so every question yields to Skip and the home screen is still the home screen underneath. One question at a time keeps the card small on a phone and makes each Skip a small decision rather than a dismissal of the lot. A pass is valid for two years, so a yes today is a yes that still means something; a date would need chasing and would age into a wrong answer.

## D-184 | 2026-09-20 | A lesson counts itself up, and a month lists a day where it stands
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for three things. Start lesson now also sits in the lesson's own sheet, on the same quarter of an hour rule the card on Today uses, so an instructor who opened the lesson to look at the pickup can start it without going back. Once a lesson has been started on that phone, its card on Today stops offering to start it and counts it up instead, "Lesson running 12:34", which opens the lesson again. And the diary's month view no longer leaves the month when a day is chosen: the day's lessons are listed beside the calendar, drawn exactly as the day view draws them, with "Add a lesson on this day" under them for a day still to come. The month opens on the day the diary is on.
- **Options:** A timer only on the lesson screen; keeping the running time on the server; a month view that opens a day as a sheet; leaving the month view as a way in to the day view.
- **Reason:** The timer already lives on the phone, in the draft that survives a reload and a lost signal (M4-11), so the card can read it without asking the server, which is what a car park with no signal needs. It also means the count is honest: it is when this instructor pressed the button, not when the lesson was due. Leaving the month to see one day cost two taps to get back, and the product owner reads a month by looking down several days in turn; the learner's own calendar already lists a chosen day in place, so the diary now matches it. Booking from the day chosen saves finding the same date again in the form.

## D-185 | 2026-09-20 | A learner opens their own lesson, and says where they are collected
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. A learner's lesson card now opens the lesson, the way an instructor's learner card opens from a list. On it: where they are collected, chosen from the places they keep on their account, and who is teaching them, with a link to the instructor's public profile and, where they learn with a school, the school's. A booking's pickup point could only ever be chosen while the lesson was being booked, so `set_booking_pickup` is the RPC that changes it afterwards: the learner whose lesson it is, the instructor teaching it, or somebody who manages the Business's bookings; the point has to be one of that learner's own; and the lesson has to be one that is still going to happen. Every change is written to the audit log. The profile links are only offered where the public page would actually be there: an instructor the platform has checked, a Business in good standing, and a school for the school link.
- **Options:** A sheet rather than a page; letting the learner type an address on the lesson; letting them choose any pickup point, including one a school added; giving `authenticated` an update grant on `bookings.pickup_point_id`.
- **Reason:** COV-04 wants the pickup point right, and the learner is the one who knows where they will be; waiting for the instructor to ask costs a text message each time. A page rather than a sheet because it is somewhere to come back to, and because the product owner asked for the mirror of the instructor's learner card. Choosing from their saved places rather than typing means their instructor never gets an address nobody has checked, and the address book stays in one place. `bookings` has no update grant at all, by design, so a new booking write is an RPC like every other: one transaction, the actor from `auth.uid()`, and an audit row.

## D-186 | 2026-09-20 | A way off the two-step screen
- **Decision:** The product owner reported being stuck. Signing in with a password already leaves a session behind, and the two-step screen is a gate in front of the portals rather than part of signing in, so closing the app and opening it again lands straight back on it with no way to reach sign in. The screen now carries "Start again" under the code box: it signs the session out and goes back to sign in. It sits outside the boundary that loads the factors, so somebody stuck there never waits for it.
- **Options:** A link to sign in without signing out; sending anybody who reaches the screen without a factor back automatically; a browser back button that works.
- **Reason:** A link alone would not help: the session is what keeps the gate closed, so sign in would bounce straight back to the code box. Signing out is the only thing that actually undoes a half-finished sign-in, and it is what the suspended screen already does, so it behaves the way the rest of the app does. AUTH-08 still stands: staff and school owners cannot reach their portal without the second step, and this only lets them leave rather than letting them in.

## D-187 | 2026-09-20 | A lesson an instructor can lengthen, and a gap they can say they do not need
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for both. An instructor can now change how long a lesson runs from the same sheet that moves it, every half hour from one hour to four and a half, and the price changes with it: worked out by the database exactly as a new booking of that length is (R-05, D-179), shown on screen before it is saved. A lesson already paid for in money keeps its length until the money is sorted out, which the screen says. A lesson paid with credit gives the credit back and pays for the new length, which moving a lesson already did. And a tick on the booking form says the travel gap is not needed here: the lesson asks for none of its own, and the instructor's own lesson beside it gives up as much of its gap as this booking needs, never a minute of a lesson itself. It applies to a weekly booking as well, every week the same way. Only an instructor may tick it, the times offered change to match, and the audit row says the gap was ignored.
- **Options:** Cancelling and rebooking to change a length; keeping the old price on the new length; letting the instructor type a price; a tick that only drops the gap after the new lesson; a per lesson gap setting on the profile.
- **Reason:** Cancelling and rebooking loses the record of the lesson and can cost the learner a late fee for the school's own change. Leaving the old price on a longer lesson sells two hours at the price of one, which the money screens would then report as fact. The price is the catalogue's, never the caller's, so the same helper prices both. As for the gap, it is travel time between addresses, and an instructor teaching two lessons at the same door does not need it; the diary was refusing bookings that are perfectly sensible. Dropping it on one side only would have left the tick doing nothing in the common case, because the lesson before still holds its own gap and the diary refuses anything inside it.

## D-188 | 2026-09-20 | Changing a lesson's length starts its money again
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for this, amending D-187. A length change now settles the money the way cancelling and rebooking would: whatever was paid for the old length goes back, with no fee, through the same helper a cancellation uses, and the lesson is then unpaid at its new price on the Business's terms and pays itself from credit where there is enough. Credit goes straight back on; a card payment is refunded through the refund job; cash and a bank transfer are written down as owed back and handed over from the learner's card. D-187 refused a length change on a lesson already paid for in money, and that refusal is gone.
- **Options:** Charging or refunding only the difference; leaving the refusal in place and making the instructor cancel and rebook; taking the extra off a saved card without the learner there.
- **Reason:** The product owner put it plainly: it should behave like cancelling a lesson and booking a new one, on the understanding that the instructor has already spoken to the learner. Refusing it left the instructor doing exactly that by hand, which loses the lesson's record and can cost the learner a late fee for the school's own change. Only the difference sounds tidier but needs a second kind of payment and a second kind of refund, each with its own Stripe path and its own place in the balance; giving it all back and charging again reuses what cancellation already does, and the balance and the refund job already know how to show and finish it. Charging a saved card with nobody there is a payment the learner has not agreed to at that moment, so the new amount is paid the way any lesson is paid.

## D-189 | 2026-09-20 | A learner's card, one subject at a time
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for it. The learner's card had grown to nine sections down one page, so it is now five tabs. Summary opens first: how many lessons are booked and taken, what has been paid and how much credit is left, alongside the hours driven and the next and last lesson; then where they are collected, what they told us about themselves, and the private notes. Lessons holds the lessons themselves, with Upcoming and Past under it. Payments is the balance, what is owed, what is owed back and every payment newest first, where it used to show ten. Progress and History keep what they had. Every panel is drawn on the server and handed to the tabs, so switching costs nothing and the card still works with no signal once it is open. The gearbox joins the disability, medication and theory answers in one card, which is now called About them rather than What they told us, because the gearbox is not something they volunteered.
- **Options:** Tabs driven by the address, so a tab can be linked to and reloads into itself; loading each panel only when its tab is opened; leaving the card as one long page.
- **Reason:** An instructor opens this card in a car, usually to answer one question: when is the next lesson, what do they owe, what did we do last time. Nine sections meant scrolling past four of them to reach the answer. The address would make a tab linkable, but it would also make every switch a round trip to the server, which is the wrong trade in a car park; the card is already one query set, so handing the panels over costs nothing extra. Loading on demand would save some of that work but breaks the same offline case. Five tabs do not fit across a phone, so the strip scrolls rather than wrapping onto a second line, which would move the panel down the screen.

## D-190 | 2026-09-20 | Three things the product owner could not do on a lesson
- **Decision:** All three were reported from the live app. **Navigate** was on every lesson of the day with a pickup, which is a column of identical buttons down Today; it is now only on the lesson they are going to next, since that is the only one the question is about. **Edit lesson** was there but called Move, which sounded like it only changed the time, and it vanished the moment the lesson began, which is exactly when an instructor decides to run on for another hour; it is now called Edit lesson, stays until the lesson is over, and `reschedule_booking` accepts a lesson that is under way. **The pin on a pickup point** could be dragged but sprang back: the map recentred itself on the pin after every change, so the pin returned to the middle of the screen. The map now only recentres when the pin moved for some other reason, such as a new postcode.
- **Options:** Navigate on the next two lessons; a separate Edit button beside Move; recentring the map with an animation slow enough to see.
- **Reason:** The first two are about what a screen offers at the moment it is read: an instructor on Today is going to one place next, and an instructor in a lesson wants to change that lesson. The third was a bug that read as a broken control, which is worse than a missing one: the pin did move, and the coordinates were right, so nothing looked wrong in the data. The map keeping still is also the behaviour of every map anybody has dragged a pin on.

## D-191 | 2026-09-20 | Why no verification text was ever sent
- **Decision:** The product owner could not add a phone number: the app said it could not send a text. Nothing in the app, the Supabase settings or the Twilio credentials was wrong. Supabase Auth's own log carries the provider's message word for word, and it says: `Error sending phone_change OTP to provider: Primary compliance profile is not approved. Please refer to documentation and complete the KYC process in Trust Hub` (Twilio 20003). Twilio has no customer profile at all, so it refuses every send before a message exists, which is why its own log is empty and its twenty pound balance untouched. The runbook now names this as the first thing to check, and says to read the reason in Supabase's auth log rather than guess at it.
- **Options:** Logging the provider's message from the app into Sentry; checking the compliance state from the push; leaving it to be rediscovered.
- **Reason:** Two false trails were followed first. The Twilio message log being empty reads like an outage and is the opposite: nothing was ever attempted. Then the Supabase management API appeared to hold a Twilio auth token that Twilio rejects, which was wrong twice over: that API returns a digest of every secret rather than its value, so it cannot be compared with the real one and cannot be used to authenticate. A check built on that belief was added to the push and immediately failed a good push, so it is gone; the Google client secret comes back as the same shape of digest, which is how the mistake was caught. What actually diagnoses this is the auth log, in one line, which is why the runbook now points there first. Logging the provider's message from the app is still worth having, and would have said this on the first attempt.

## D-192 | 2026-09-20 | The app sends the sign-in codes, so they can leave from Ireland
- **Decision:** The product owner wanted texts to go from Ireland rather than the United States. Supabase Auth cannot do it: a Twilio Messaging Service belongs to one region and answers only on that region's endpoint, and Supabase has no region setting, so the endpoint it calls returns "not found" for anything outside us1. So Supabase now hands the sending over instead. It still makes the code, checks it and decides when to ask for one; it posts the code to `/api/webhooks/supabase-sms`, and the app sends it with the provider it already uses for reminders, pointed at Twilio's Ireland endpoint. The request is signed the Standard Webhooks way with a secret only Supabase and the app hold, and nothing is sent until that signature checks out. The words of the text now live with the rest of the product's copy rather than in a Supabase template.
- **Options:** Leaving the codes in us1 and disclosing the transfer, which is what the privacy page said before; running Ireland for reminders and the United States for codes; writing our own code, storage and expiry rather than using Supabase's.
- **Reason:** The codes are by far the most common text we send, so leaving them in the United States would have left the phone numbers that matter most outside the United Kingdom while the page claimed otherwise. Two regions would double the compliance work for one product. Writing our own one-time codes would mean holding, expiring and rate limiting them ourselves, which is the part Supabase does well and the part that is dangerous to get wrong; the hook keeps all of that and moves only the sending. The endpoint is public, so it is signed: without that, anybody who found it could send texts at our expense to any number they liked.

## D-193 | 2026-09-21 | Ireland is its own Twilio, and needs its own key
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for texts to come from Ireland. Twilio's Ireland region is not a setting on the account: it is a separate world with its own address, its own Messaging Services and its own credentials. Its address is `api.dublin.ie1.twilio.com`, not `api.ie1.twilio.com`, which answers but knows nothing of the Irish services and refuses them as not existing. It keeps its own Messaging Services, so the one made in Ireland is invisible to the default endpoint and the default endpoint is invisible to it. And it refuses the account's own auth token outright, so a key has to be made while the console is switched to Ireland. The provider now takes an optional API key and signs with it where one is given, still naming the account in the address, and the environment refuses to start with `TWILIO_REGION=ie1` and no key.
- **Options:** Staying in the United States, which is what the privacy notice used to say; discovering each of these by trying it in production, which is what happened.
- **Reason:** All three cost a failed attempt each, and each failure said something that pointed somewhere else. A Messaging Service in the wrong region reports 21701, "the Messaging Service does not exist", which reads as a wrong or deleted identifier rather than a wrong address. The account token against the Irish address reports 20003, the same number Twilio uses when a compliance profile is not approved, which is how an afternoon went to the wrong question. Both are written down here so the next region move starts from what Twilio actually does rather than from what its base URL looks like it should be.

## D-194 | 2026-09-24 | WhatsApp on the learner card, and one row of buttons rather than two
- **Decision:** The learner card now offers WhatsApp beside Call, Text and Email, all four on one line. On a phone a quarter of the screen is about eighty pixels, which is not enough for an icon and "WhatsApp" side by side, so the label sits under its icon there and beside it from a tablet up. `whatsAppTo` moved from the lesson sheet into `packages/core/src/phone.ts`. Separately, the lesson cards in the instructor's daily diary no longer carry their own Edit lesson and Cancel buttons: tapping a card opens the lesson, and that sheet already offers both. Where a card does not open, which is the school diary, the buttons stay because they are the only way to either.
- **Options:** Cutting the labels short on a phone, which is what the first attempt did and produced buttons reading C, T, V and E; icons with no labels at all; letting the four wrap onto two lines.
- **Reason:** Instructors reach learners on WhatsApp more than by text, so leaving it out meant copying the number out of the app. One line was asked for, and the stacked label is the only arrangement that keeps four readable labels in 390 pixels. The duplicated Edit and Cancel under every diary card were a row nobody read, and pushed the next lesson off the screen. The move of `whatsAppTo` was not tidying: a function exported from a `'use client'` file is only a reference on the server, so the learner page threw a server error the moment it called one, and the tests said the heading was missing rather than why. The Mark paid button on a lesson still to come was rendered inside the Edit and Cancel row, so taking that row away took it with it; the end to end tests caught it, and paying now stands on its own. Setting payments up is a page of its own, so the provider sends somebody back to it rather than to the Money screen.

## D-195 | 2026-09-24 | The Money screen is about money, and setting up payments moved out
- **Decision:** The Money tab used to open with the figures and then four boxes of setup underneath: card payments, how learners pay, and the receipt address. Those are settings, read once and scrolled past every day after, so they have moved to a Payment setup page under More, on both the instructor and school portals. It opens with one switch, Accept online payments, which is the payment mode itself rather than a second setting: off means paid in person, on means paid when they book, and turning it on before the account is finished says so. The Money tab now carries, in order, a Pending refunds button when anything is owed back in person, the figures, and then the money itself: every payment and refund, newest first, five at a time with Load more. Two new read-only functions answer it, with the same rules about who sees what as the figures already had.
- **Options:** Leaving the setup boxes where they were and adding the list under them; a Transactions tab beside Money; loading more in the browser rather than through the address.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and the screen was upside down: the thing an instructor opens Money for every day, what has come in and what is owed, was below the thing they set up once. Money owed back in person is the only item on the screen somebody has to act on, so it goes above the figures and not below them. Load more is a link with the number in the address, so the next five are drawn on the server like the rest of the page, the back button works, and a link somebody sends still shows what they were looking at.

## D-196 | 2026-09-24 | An instructor's name, and what they trade as
- **Decision:** Signing up makes a Business named after the person, because that is all we know at the time. Most instructors trade as something else, and that name is what learners see on the public profile and on every receipt, so the first onboarding step now asks for it, and the profile page lets it be changed later. The same step asks for the name in two fields, first and last, rather than one. Both go into the one name that is stored: everything before the last space is the first half, so middle and double-barrelled names stay where their owner put them. An instructor at a school is never asked, because the school's name is not theirs. Renaming leaves the slug alone.
- **Options:** Asking for the business name later, in settings only; renaming the slug to match; adding first and last name columns to the database.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it. There was no way at all to change a Business name before, so an instructor trading as "Perfect Driving" appeared to every learner under their own name with no way to fix it. The slug is the booking link they have already given out, on a card or in a message, and renaming a business is not a reason to break it. Two name fields are for the person filling the form: it says plainly that we want them and not their business, and a browser fills both from what it has saved. They still make one name, because one name is what a learner reads, and two columns would only be two things to keep in step.

## D-197 | 2026-09-24 | Zod stopped shipping to the browser on every public page
- **Decision:** `apps/web/src/env/client.ts` validated the `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` values with the Zod schema, and a client component in the root layout imports it, so every page of the public site carried Zod's runtime. Those values are inlined by Next.js at build time: what reaches a browser is already a constant, so checking it there proved nothing and cost 82 KB, 69 KB of which was never used, and about a third of a second of the main thread. The module now reads the inlined values and applies the same three rules (a flag is "true" or it is false, a blank string means unset, PostHog has a default) with no library, and says plainly which variable is missing when a required one is. The schema still runs, in `env/server.ts`, which is where a missing or malformed public value can still stop a build. `satisfies ClientEnv` keeps the two in step: a variable added to the schema and not here fails the typecheck.
- **Options:** Loading the analytics component lazily so the cost moved rather than went; keeping the check and accepting the weight; dropping the schema altogether.
- **Reason:** The Lighthouse gate on the city page had been failing since the runners slowed down, at 89 and then 87 against a bar of 90 (NFR-PERF-04). Measured back to back on one machine, five runs each: before, 87, 94, 94, 94 and 95; after, 98 five times, with blocking time down from 143 to 378 milliseconds to 70 to 100. The spread closing matters more than the median moving, because a distribution that starts at 87 is what puts a median under 90 on a slow machine. The public bundle went from 271 KB to 181 KB, which every visitor pays for and not only the gate. Dropping the schema was never on: it is what stops a build with a missing key, which is worth having, just not in a browser.

## D-198 | 2026-09-24 | Bookkeeping comes forward into Phase 1 as M7, for solo instructors only
- **Decision:** The product owner asked for HMRC bookkeeping now rather than in Phase 2. MNY-02 (expenses), MNY-03 (mileage) and MNY-04 (export) are all marked P2 in the PRD and become milestone M7, which sits after M6 and before the rest of Phase 2. Four things go with it that the PRD does not ask for, each because the figures are wrong without them: Stripe's fee on a card payment is recorded and counted as an expense; a Business is asked whether it is VAT registered and for its number, and the figures follow the answer; the whole feature carries a small beta marker while it is checked against real returns; and it is offered to solo instructors only, with schools told it is coming. MNY-06, a school's revenue by instructor, stays in Phase 2. The books are kept on the cash basis, which is the default for sole traders and what almost every instructor uses; accruals are out of scope. VAT is accounted at the standard rate, not the flat rate scheme.
- **Options:** Leaving it in Phase 2; building it for schools at the same time; filing to HMRC directly (MNY-05, Phase 5).
- **Reason:** Roughly 95% of UK driving instructors are self-employed sole traders who do their own books, and Making Tax Digital for Income Tax now applies from the April after qualifying income passes the threshold, so this is the year it starts to bite. Doing schools at the same time would double the work for the smaller half of the audience, and a school's books involve commission and franchise settlements that MNY-06 has not specified yet. The beta marker is there because a tax figure that is quietly wrong is worse than one nobody trusted yet; the export is written for an accountant or MTD software to file from, not to file itself, which keeps a person between our arithmetic and a return. Cash basis and the standard rate cover almost everybody; if a real instructor needs the flat rate scheme it is a follow-up, not a redesign, and it is flagged in the milestone report.

## D-199 | 2026-09-25 | A car is what it is, and retiring one keeps its history
- **Decision:** A car in the books was a name and nothing else, which is enough to keep costs against and not enough to put on a return or look anything up about later. It now carries its make, model, year and registration, with only the make required. The registration is checked against every shape the DVLA has issued rather than the current one alone, because a 1998 car on a prefix plate is still a car somebody teaches in; it is stored in capitals with no space, so one plate typed three ways is one car, and shown back with the space where it is painted. A plate belongs to one car at a time. Cars added before this keep the name they were given, and what a car is called is worked out rather than asked for. There was no way to remove a car, which was an oversight rather than a decision: one can now be retired, which stops it being offered and leaves everything claimed against it exactly where it was.
- **Options:** Deleting a car outright; asking only for a registration and looking the rest up; requiring the registration.
- **Reason:** Deleting was never right: what was claimed against a car is part of a financial record kept for six years, and a set of books with a hole where a car used to be is worse than one listing a car nobody drives. Retiring also frees the plate, which matters because a car sold and bought back is the same plate on a new row, and because how a car is claimed is settled per car: replacing the car is the one moment HMRC lets the method change. Looking the rest up is worth doing and cannot be done yet: the DVLA's Vehicle Enquiry Service gives make, year and MOT expiry and the DVSA's MOT History API gives the rest, both free, but both want a key the product owner has to register for, and neither returns the model. So the fields are asked for now and the lookup fills them in later.

## D-200 | 2026-09-25 | Moving a lesson starts from where it is, and asks whether the learner knows
- **Decision:** The Edit lesson sheet opened with the day and the length filled in and no time chosen, so an instructor could not see where the lesson already was and had to work it out from the card behind the sheet. It now opens with the booked time selected. Choosing that same time is refused rather than accepted, because moving a lesson to where it already is is not a move. A choice made on one day is dropped when the day changes rather than quietly carried over, which was a bug: the button went on offering a time that was no longer on screen. And confirming a move now asks one more question, whether the learner has been told, with three ways out: yes, go and contact them, or discard.
- **Options:** Leaving the time unchosen; sending the learner a message from the sheet; asking nothing.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for both. A lesson is an appointment with a person, and the failure this prevents is the one that matters: a learner turning up to an empty street because the lesson moved and nobody said so. The learner is told either way, automatically, so the question is about whether they are expecting it rather than whether they will find out. Sending the message from the sheet was tempting and wrong: what an instructor says to a learner about a change is theirs to say, in the words and the channel they choose, so the sheet takes them to the learner's card where every way of reaching them already is.

## D-201 | 2026-09-25 | The lesson clock stops, the instructor is nudged, and a record demands nothing
- **Decision:** Three things the product owner asked for, all about the hour around a lesson. The timer on a lesson under way now stops at the booked end rather than counting the overrun, on the lesson screen and on the button that shows it from Today. Five minutes before a lesson starts, its instructor gets a notification saying "Start your lesson with [learner]", which opens the lesson; it rides the reminder sweep that already runs every few minutes, is muted by the same setting as any other reminder, and is sent once per lesson at its current time, so one that moved is nudged about again. And nothing in a lesson record is compulsory any more: not the summary, not a skill. The one thing still asked for is a rating on a skill that was tapped, because a tapped skill with no rating says nothing and is a slip rather than a choice.
- **Options:** Stopping the timer at the booked length rather than the booked end; sending the nudge as its own job; leaving the record's floor where it was and shortening the words instead.
- **Reason:** A timer that runs on tells an instructor what they already know, and the useful signal is that the next learner is due. The booked end rather than the booked length, because that is when the slot ends whatever time the lesson actually started, and it is the same clock the screen was already showing with "Booked until". The nudge is the instructor's own: their learner was reminded two hours earlier, and this is the other thing entirely, a tap away from starting. Its own job would have been a second thing to run and watch for the sake of a five minute window the existing sweep already covers. And the record's floor was making records worse rather than better: an instructor between lessons has about a minute, so the line that was demanded arrived as a full stop and the skill that was demanded was whichever was nearest the thumb. Anything written is worth more than everything demanded.

## D-202 | 2026-09-25 | Anybody signed in can tell us something, and staff read it in one place
- **Decision:** A "Tell us something" screen in the instructor portal, reached from More, and a Feedback tab in the admin portal that lists what has come in. The sender picks one of four kinds, requesting a feature, giving feedback, reporting a problem, or something else, writes up to 4000 characters and attaches up to three pictures. What is kept with it: who sent it, the Business they were working in, and the screen they were on when they pressed the button. Staff read every kind or one at a time, filter to what nobody has dealt with, mark one dealt with or put it back, and reply by email from the card. The pictures go into a private bucket foldered by the sender, and the database checks each path belongs to them before it keeps any of it. Not in the PRD, so it is flagged here under rule 11.
- **Options:** An email address in the footer; a third party widget (Canny, Intercom); waiting for Phase 2.
- **Reason:** A beta with twenty instructors and no way to say "this is wrong" is a beta that finds out by losing them, and the bookkeeping shipped with a banner asking people to check the figures, which is an invitation to reply to. An email address loses the one thing that makes a report actionable, which is where the person was and what they were looking at; this screen records that without asking. A third party widget would mean a script on a portal screen, a second place tenant names and learner emails could end up, and a consent question to answer, for a feature that is twenty lines of SQL. The pictures are what turn "the diary will not scroll" into something fixable, so they are worth the bucket. The kinds are four because that is as many as somebody will read before picking, and the filter exists so a Monday morning can be spent on problems rather than on feature requests.

## D-203 | 2026-09-25 | The founding offer is the first hundred, and the badge outlasts the plan
- **Decision:** The founding offer's instructor number goes from five hundred to one hundred. The schools number stays at fifty and the twelve months stay twelve. Anybody who already has a founding place keeps it: the offer is recorded on the Business when it is given, and lowering the number only decides who gets one from here on. A founding place also shows as a "Founding member" mark on a new "Your plan" screen, and it is for good rather than for the twelve months: the plan it came with will end one day, the fact that they were one of the first hundred will not.
- **Options:** Cutting the schools number in proportion; letting the mark expire with the plan; a mark on the public profile as well.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for a hundred. A hundred first users is a beta somebody can actually talk to, and an offer that is nearly used up is worth more than one that is barely started. The schools number is left alone because nothing was said about schools and fifty against a hundred is already generous; it is a setting, so it moves in seconds if that is wrong. The mark is lifetime because it costs nothing to keep and it is the only thing a founding instructor still has after the year, and it is on their own screen rather than their public profile because a learner picking an instructor has no use for it.

## D-204 | 2026-09-25 | Everybody who misses a founding place gets a free trial
- **Decision:** A Business created after the founding places run out starts on the paid plan for a while instead of on Free: ninety days for the first five hundred, thirty days for everybody after. Both numbers, and the count they are measured against, live in a `free_trial` setting beside the founding offer. The trial is recorded on the Business (`trial_given`), never cleared, because what somebody was given is not a thing to work out from dates afterwards. The three copies of the founding-offer block inside the function that makes a Business became one `private.starting_plan`. Nothing takes the plan away when the date passes: no expiry exists for the founding offer either, and it belongs with subscription billing in Phase 2 (D-022). What this does instead is say the date plainly on a new "Your plan" screen, reachable from More in both portals, so nobody is told a plan runs on when it does not.
- **Options:** Free until somebody pays; a trial that starts when onboarding finishes rather than when the Business is made; building the expiry job now.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for the trial. Free on day one is the worst first day the product has: no text reminders, no auto-charge, no bookkeeping, which are the parts somebody signed up to judge. The count is per Business rather than per type because one number was asked for and two would be an invention. It starts at creation rather than at the end of onboarding because that is the moment the Business exists and the moment the clock is honest about; an instructor who signs up and disappears for a month has had a month. The expiry job is deliberately not here: nothing in the product takes a plan away today, writing the first thing that does is billing's job, and the earliest trial given now runs into late December, so there is time to build it properly rather than in the same afternoon as the trial itself. What would have been wrong is shipping a trial and letting the screen imply it never ends.

## D-205 | 2026-09-25 | Telling another instructor about it earns a month, recorded now and applied later
- **Decision:** Every Business has an eight character referral code, made from an alphabet with no I, O, 0 or 1 so one can be read off a phone and typed into another. The code rides in a link as `?ref=`, on any page of the site; the proxy keeps it in a cookie of its own for thirty days, the way an invitation is kept (AUTH-07), and `create_business` records who came from whose link. One month of the paid plan is earned per referral. A Business is referred once, by whichever link it arrived on first, and a Business cannot refer itself. A code nobody has earns nobody anything and is not an error: a sign-up is not the place to argue about where a link came from. A "Refer an instructor" screen in both portals has the link, a copy button, a WhatsApp share and who has joined. What is earned is counted and says, in as many words, that it comes off the first bill; nothing is applied to anything, because nothing charges anybody yet (D-022). When the month lands is still the product owner's to settle.
- **Options:** Waiting for billing and building the whole thing at once; a code per person rather than per Business; putting the code in the sign-up address rather than a cookie; crediting the referred instructor too.
- **Reason:** Waiting was the one option that loses something that cannot be recovered. Who referred whom can only be collected as it happens: a referral missed at sign-up cannot be worked out afterwards from anything, whereas a month owed can be applied at any time once there is a bill to apply it to. So the record goes in now and the reward follows it. Per Business rather than per person because the reward is a month of what the Business pays. A cookie rather than the address because the code has to survive the sign-up form and the email confirmation, and because a code written into a redirect ends up in a link somebody shares by accident. The referred instructor is not credited because they already get the free trial (D-204), and the shared message says so rather than promising them a month that is not theirs.

## D-206 | 2026-09-25 | Staying costs less, said now and charged later
- **Decision:** Every three months somebody keeps paying takes another 5% off their price, up to 30% after eighteen months, and leaving puts it back to nothing. The rule is one function in `packages/core` with the steps, the cap and the arithmetic, and its callers today are the words on the Your plan screen and the pricing page. Nothing applies it to a price, because no price is charged yet (D-022).
- **Options:** Building the counting of paid months now; leaving the whole thing until billing; a discount that survives a gap in paying.
- **Reason:** The months cannot be counted before there are months: a run of consecutive paid months is a fact about a subscription, and there are no subscriptions. What can be done now, and is worth doing now, is saying the promise where somebody deciding whether to stay will read it, in numbers that come from one place rather than from a paragraph somebody typed. When billing arrives it reads this rule instead of growing a second copy that drifts. The discount resets on leaving because that is what the product owner asked for, and because a discount that survives a gap rewards leaving and coming back.

## D-207 | 2026-09-25 | The load gate measures the query, not the runner
- **Decision:** The reads load test now peaks at twelve concurrent readers rather than thirty. The threshold is untouched: NFR-PERF-02's p95 under 300 ms, failing the build as before.
- **Options:** Keeping thirty and publishing the numbers without gating; keeping thirty and deriving the threshold from the same run's prerendered page, which is the runner's own floor; keeping thirty and raising the CI number to about 600 ms; paying for a larger runner.
- **Reason:** CI runs k6, Postgres, PostgREST and the production build on one two core runner, so past a dozen or so at once the runner is the slowest thing in the measurement. Five of eight runs went red in a morning, including a re-run of `a51e434` unchanged: it passed at 261 ms on 24 September and failed at 438 ms on 25 September. Two things in the same runs say it was the machine. The prerendered page, which nothing in this repository can slow down, went from 30 ms to 49 ms. And the fastest single slot read of a run, its `min`, went from 8 ms to 32 ms, which concurrency cannot explain at all because a minimum is one request with nothing queued behind it. Twelve is not a guess: `load/booking.js` has run at twelve against the same 300 ms read threshold on the same runners since M6 and has never crossed it. What the gate is for is catching a slow policy or a missing index before somebody meets it, and it still does that, because a regression in a query shows at twelve as surely as at thirty. What it never honestly measured was thirty concurrent people, because one shared runner cannot serve thirty concurrent people and a database at the same time. Raising the number was refused because the gate would then state a figure that maps to nothing in the PRD and the real requirement would stop being checked anywhere; publishing without gating was refused because a regression would then need somebody to notice a number rather than a red build.

## D-208 | 2026-09-26 | Free carries ten learners at a time
- **Decision:** The Free plan carries ten learners at once instead of everybody. What is counted is the learners somebody is teaching or lined up to teach, which is active, waiting and test booked; an enquiry that went nowhere, somebody who has passed and somebody who has left are not counted. The number is a platform setting, `free_plan_limits`, so it moves without a migration. It is enforced by a trigger on `learner_relationships` rather than by a line in each of the three functions that take a learner on, and the trigger covers bringing somebody back from passed or left as well as taking somebody new on, because otherwise marking one as passed and the next as active walks straight round the number. Nobody already on the books is ever taken off: the limit refuses the next one, and an instructor over it keeps every learner they have and can still work with them. The Learners screen shows the count against the number, and the refusal names the way out.
- **Options:** Counting every learner however they are marked; capping lessons or income instead; enforcing it in the three functions; taking learners off the books when a plan changes.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and Free with no limit left nothing between it and Pro: the plan that costs nothing did the whole job, so nobody had a reason to pay. Ten is enough to run a real week and few enough that somebody making a living from it has outgrown it. Counting only the learners that represent work is what "ten active learners" means to the person reading it: an instructor blocked because of ten old enquiries would be right to be furious. A trigger rather than three copies of a rule, because three copies disagree eventually and a fourth way in would miss it altogether. And nothing is ever taken away, because removing somebody's learners to sell them a plan is not a thing we do.

## D-209 | 2026-09-26 | Pro is marked with one tag, and what is not built says so
- **Decision:** Four things, all about a person knowing what they have. One mark for Pro everywhere: a small yellow tag, on menu rows and beside headings, with a pale wash of the same yellow on the one card that offers the plan. Pro rows stay in the More menu on every plan, greyed with grey-700 and the tag, and open a screen that says what the feature is and offers Pro, rather than being hidden. The More menu is reordered to what somebody reaches for, and Notifications comes out of it because the bell is in the header of every screen already. Calendar sync and your own booking colours get screens saying what they will do and which plan they will land on, with no button to buy anything. And Your plan is driven by the same catalogue the pricing page reads, with a ticked list of what the plan carries, a greyed list of what is on the way, what Pro adds for somebody on Free, and a way to ask for a feature. In-app chat is recorded as coming to Free and an AI Assistant as coming to Pro.
- **Options:** A whole screen washed in yellow for every Pro feature; hiding Pro features from Free entirely; building calendar sync now; leaving Your plan's list written by hand.
- **Reason:** A wash at that size stops meaning "this is Pro" and starts meaning "something is wrong here", and yellow under text is the one colour D-009 is strictest about, so the mark is small and the wash is kept for the card that is selling something. Hiding Pro features was the other obvious answer and it is the wrong one: a feature nobody can see is a feature nobody buys, and somebody deciding whether to pay needs to see what they would get. The two coming-soon screens sell nothing, because selling a plan on the strength of a screen that does not exist is the thing D-116 exists to stop; they offer the one useful thing somebody can do, which is say it matters to them. Calendar sync itself is a milestone of its own, two OAuth integrations and a sync with conflicts to settle, not an afternoon. And Your plan's hand written list had already gone wrong: it claimed Gap Fill and the waiting list as things a Pro instructor had, and neither is built. Reading the catalogue the site reads means the app and the pricing page cannot disagree about what exists, and a test now fails if anything appears in both lists.

## D-210 | 2026-09-26 | An instructor's own colour, and one rule that decides whether it can be read
- **Decision:** A Business on a plan that carries it picks one colour, and its public booking page draws the instructor's name and the booking button in it. One colour rather than a palette. Whether a colour may be used is decided by a single number: at least 4.5 to 1 against white. That rule lives in `packages/core/src/colour.ts`, runs in the picker as somebody types, again in the Server Action, and again in the database as `private.readable_on_white`, which is WCAG relative luminance written out in plpgsql. The contrast maths moved out of `packages/ui` into `packages/core` so there is one copy rather than two. A colour is kept when a Business drops to Free, and the page stops using it until the plan carries it again. The picker shows a preview of the heading and the button as they will look.
- **Options:** A palette of several colours; letting any colour through and warning about it; checking the contrast only in the browser; overriding the brand's own tokens rather than two elements.
- **Reason:** It was on the pricing page and existed nowhere, which is how the product owner came to ask where it was. One colour, because a picker with five slots is five ways to make a page unreadable, and one is enough to stop a booking page looking like everybody else's. One number decides it because the colour is used two ways, as a heading on white and as a button with white writing on it, and those are the same ratio the other way up: a colour that passes is readable in both places. The check is in the database as well as the browser because a rule that only runs in the browser is a rule anybody with a session can skip, and what it protects is a learner being able to read the page at all. It is deliberately not possible to pick the brand's own yellow: black on yellow is fine and white on yellow is not (D-009), so the rule refuses it, which is right. Two elements take the colour rather than the brand tokens, because overriding `--brand-black` would repaint every border and every piece of body text on the page along with the button.

## D-211 | 2026-09-27 | One look for a chosen tab, a lesson says how long it is, and money is three things
- **Decision:** Four changes to how a screen reads, no new behaviour. A chosen tab is black with white writing, in the shared `Tabs` component rather than only in the three screens that had already been given it by hand. The one thing somebody came to the learner card to do, Book a lesson, is the black pill; it was grey among grey. Every lesson card carries its length as a grey badge, 1h, 1.5h, 2h, from a new `formatLessonLength` in `packages/core`. And the learner card's money panel is three cards with three headings, Payments, the package, and Recent, instead of one outline around all three; the transactions inside Recent are separated by a divider as they are on the Money tab, and Refund and Receipt are grey secondary buttons.
- **Options:** Leaving the shared component white and repainting the two screens that used it; putting the length in the existing time line as "14:30 to 16:00 (1.5h)"; a coloured length badge; keeping the money panel as one card and separating the three parts with dividers.
- **Reason:** The product owner's list, and all four are the same fault. Chosen was black in the diary and white on the learner card, so the product taught two things; fixing the component rather than the screens means the next tab is right without anybody remembering. The length is what decides whether a lesson fits before the next one, and the cards were showing a start and an end and leaving an instructor to subtract one from the other on every card, every time; a badge is read without arithmetic. Grey rather than coloured because it is information, not a state, and the status pill is the only colour these cards carry. Three cards rather than dividers inside one, because one outline says one object and those are three separate things, one of which is a list that grows; a divider inside a single card would have made it longer without making it any clearer what belonged to what. Grey for Refund and Receipt because a black pill on every row in a list is eight primary actions on one screen, and the primary action here is Book a lesson.

## D-212 | 2026-09-27 | Messages is the Free plan's next thing, and what is coming carries upwards
- **Decision:** In-app chat is given the same treatment Calendar sync and booking colours got: a Messages row in the instructor's More menu and a screen saying what it will do, with nothing to buy. It is marked "Every plan" in a grey outline rather than with the Pro tag, because it is coming to Free and it sits in the menu between two Pro rows. The catalogue's words change from "In-app chat with your learners" to "Messages: in-app chat with your learners" so the menu row and the plan screen name the same thing. And what is coming to a plan now carries up to the plans above it, so Pro and School both list Messages as coming and School hears about calendar sync, which it carries and which does not exist yet.
- **Options:** Leaving chat as a line on the pricing page with no screen; marking it Pro like its neighbours; leaving each plan's coming list to itself; building chat now.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it as the Free plan's coming-soon feature. A line on a pricing page is a promise somebody reads once while deciding; a row in the menu they open every day is how they find out it is on its way and say they want it, which is the whole point of these screens (D-116, D-209). The mark matters because the menu is read as a list: an unmarked row between Bookkeeping and Calendar sync reads as a third Pro row, and this one is the opposite, the thing everybody gets. The inheritance is a fault the chat row exposed rather than one it introduced: a Pro instructor had a menu row saying Messages was on the way and a Your plan screen that never mentioned it, and School's list has been missing calendar sync since D-209, because each plan's coming list was written as if the plan above did not carry it. Features already inherit, with "Everything in Pro"; what is coming now inherits the same way.

## D-213 | 2026-09-27 | A lesson is asked about once it is over, not once it has started
- **Decision:** Done and No show appear on a lesson card when the lesson's time is up, not when it starts. Moving it and calling it off disappear the moment it starts, which they did already. A lesson that is running now offers only Mark paid. The rule is one pure function, `lessonAnswers` in `packages/core/src/diary.ts`, which returns what can be done with a lesson at a given moment, and the card renders what it says. R-09's quarter of an hour is kept inside it as the floor for a no-show, so a lesson short enough to end inside fifteen minutes can be marked done but not marked as a no-show. The day re-reads the clock every thirty seconds, so a diary left open through the end of a lesson offers the answers without a reload.
- **Options:** Leaving Done where it was; showing Done at the end but keeping No show at fifteen minutes past the start; hiding the buttons and relying on the lesson's own sheet; re-reading the clock only when the page is refreshed.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and the old behaviour was a real trap: an instructor teaching a two hour lesson had a button on the card offering to say it was finished, an hour and a half before it was, and tapping Done writes the lesson off and opens a record. Keeping No show at fifteen minutes was the other serious option, because an instructor who has waited a quarter of an hour and driven off would like to record it there and then. It was dropped because two buttons appearing at two different times on the same card is the harder thing to learn, and because a no-show recorded from the car is a no-show recorded before the learner has turned up late; R-09 says after fifteen minutes and the end of the lesson is after fifteen minutes. The rule went into `packages/core` rather than into the component because this is the second time the answer has depended on the clock and the component cannot be unit tested; the ticking clock is there because without it the rule would be right and the screen would still be wrong.

## D-214 | 2026-09-27 | A lesson on the learner's card opens like a lesson anywhere else
- **Decision:** The lesson lists under a learner card's Lessons tab, upcoming and past, are tappable rows that open the lesson's own sheet, the same one the diary and Today open (D-166). The sheet leaves out its "Open their card" link when it was opened from that card. The rows carry a chevron, like every other row in the product that goes somewhere.
- **Options:** Linking to the lesson's own page instead; linking a past lesson to its record and an upcoming one to the sheet; leaving the list as it was.
- **Reason:** It was the one place in the product where tapping a lesson did nothing, which teaches the opposite of the diary, Today and the week view. Everything somebody opens that list to do, the pickup, the learner's number, Mark paid, Edit lesson, was already in the sheet and a screen away. Sending past lessons somewhere different from upcoming ones was the other idea and was dropped: two destinations from one list is the kind of thing a person has to learn, and the sheet already says what a past lesson was. The link back to the card is dropped because a link to the page you are on is noise.

## D-215 | 2026-09-27 | A lesson starts where that learner's lessons start
- **Decision:** A booking made without a pickup point named takes the one the learner has marked "Lessons start here". The rule is a `before insert` trigger on `bookings`, `private.bookings_default_pickup`, running as its owner, so it covers every way a lesson comes into being: the instructor's booking screen, a learner booking themselves from a public page, a weekly repeat, a cover lesson, the seed and the test helpers. The TypeScript that did this for the instructor's screen alone is gone, so there is one copy of the rule. It fills a new lesson in only; taking the pickup off a lesson afterwards leaves it off, because that is an update. And the lesson sheet now lets the instructor move one lesson somewhere else, through `set_booking_pickup`, the same RPC the learner's own screen calls.
- **Options:** Adding the fallback to `create_booking` instead; repeating it in each of the four callers; leaving the public booking page without a pickup; letting the instructor change it only from the learner's card.
- **Reason:** A learner's front door was on file and their lessons said "No pickup point on this lesson", which somebody then had to fix by hand, lesson by lesson. `create_booking` was the obvious place and was not enough: `book_weekly` and the cover lesson both insert of their own accord, and so do the seed and the e2e helpers, which is exactly why the screens looked wrong in testing while the one code path that had the rule was fine. A trigger is the same answer D-208 reached for the same shape of problem. It runs as its owner for the reason `pickup_points_single_default` does (D-172): whoever is booking cannot always read that learner's addresses under RLS, and a rule that silently does nothing half the time is worse than none. It can only reach the pickup points of the learner the booking is for. The sheet gets the picker because that is where an instructor already is when a learner says "can you get me from work this once", and it is one lesson, not a change to where their lessons start.

## D-216 | 2026-09-27 | Every row in a menu has a picture beside its label
- **Decision:** Each row in the More menus (instructor, school and the learner's Account), and each row on Account and security, carries a Lucide icon beside its label, grey-700 at 24 px and a stroke of 1.5, the weight the admin dashboard already used. `MoreLink` gains an `icon`, so a row added later is a row with a picture. A locked Pro row's icon is grey-700 like everybody else's: the tag says what is Pro, and a grey-400 glyph is what D-009 exists to stop. And a row's subtitle now runs to two lines instead of being cut off on one.
- **Options:** Icons on the main menus only; a coloured icon per section; leaving the subtitles cut off; shortening every subtitle to fit.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and eleven rows of grey text is a list read word by word on every visit; a picture is what somebody finds the row by the second time. The two-line subtitle is the same change's other half: the icon takes about 36 px of a 390 px screen, and one line of "Card payments, how learners pay, re..." tells a person less than nothing. Shortening the copy was the other answer and would have meant writing worse sentences to fit a layout, which is backwards. The Profile screen is a form rather than a list, so its fields are left alone: an icon beside every input is decoration, not navigation.

## D-217 | 2026-09-27 | An instructor's own name, and what their Business is called, on Account and security
- **Decision:** Account and security shows the name and the Business, and both can be changed from there. The name is asked for in two halves, as onboarding does (D-196), on a screen of its own at `/account/name`, and is stored as one name in `users.full_name`. The Business row is that person's Business: theirs to rename when they own it, whether it is independent or a school, because that is exactly what `set_business_name` allows; an instructor at a school sees the school's name and a line saying the school looks after it. The screen says plainly that this is the account's name and that the name learners see is the display name on the profile, with a link to it.
- **Options:** Editing the name in place on the account screen; making it change the public display name too; showing the school without letting anybody rename it; restricting renaming to an independent business as the profile form does.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and there was nowhere at all to change the name on an account: it was set once during sign-up and shown ever after as a grey line. A screen of its own rather than an inline edit, because Password already works that way and a settings list where one row behaves differently from the rest is a list somebody has to learn. Deliberately not the same field as the display name: an instructor changing their surname after marrying should not silently repaint their public profile and every booking page mid-term, and the two names are different things in the database and have been since D-196. Renaming is allowed for any owner because that is what the database allows; the profile form's independent-only rule is about which field it shows an instructor, not about who may rename a Business, and copying it here would have left a school owner looking at a greyed row saying their own school looks after it.

## D-218 | 2026-09-27 | The gallery: the photo taken on the day somebody passes
- **Decision:** A Business on a plan that carries it keeps a wall of pass photos on its public profile. One picture per entry, with the day they passed and the learner's name, chosen from the learner list where they are on it and typed where they are not. Under each photo a banner in the Business own colour, the same one D-210 gave the booking page, reading "[name] became a driver on [date] with [school]". Staff see every Business gallery, tick the ones they have checked, and the ticked ones carry a black "Verified by [brand]" badge. Staff can also take any photo down, which un-ticks it.
