The instructor confirms in the form that they have the learner's permission, and when they confirmed it is stored on the row. A school's profile shows every instructor's; an instructor's profile shows their own. Nothing here is a review: no learner writes anything, nothing is scored, and none of it touches the pass rate, which has its own rule and its own threshold (R-17).
- **Options:** Several pictures per entry; publishing only what staff have ticked; a tick that could only be given and taken back, with no way to take a photo down; taking the name only from the learner list; leaving the banner in the brand's own colours.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it and it is not in the PRD, so it is logged here and flagged in the milestone report. The school keeps the same screen in its own portal rather than a read-only view, because it is one wall and a school owner should not have to ask somebody who teaches to take a photo down; the add form is there too, since a school's office often has the photo before the instructor has stopped driving. Staff reach one Business's wall from that Business's panel, which is where somebody looking into a complaint already is. It is what instructors already do on the day, posted somewhere they do not own; this puts it on the profile a learner is deciding from. Publishing on upload rather than after a check, because a wall that fills up a week after the photo was taken is a wall nobody keeps adding to, and the tick is what says we have checked rather than what says it may be seen. The hiding is the other half of that and is the part that makes publishing on upload defensible: a tick that could only be removed would leave something wrong on a public page with nothing to do about it. The typed name is there because the photo is taken on the day, and by then somebody who has passed is often marked as passed or already gone from the list, and a feature that only works for learners still on the books is a feature that works on the wrong day. A named photograph of a person on a public page is theirs, so the tick confirming permission is not optional and the moment it was given is stored: it is the answer to a complaint. The Business colour rather than the brand's, because the banner belongs to the Business and yellow with white writing is what D-009 forbids.

## D-219 | 2026-09-27 | The sweep measured the scrollbar, and scanned the development overlay
- **Decision:** Three corrections to the accessibility sweep. The 200% text check takes the scrollbar width off before deciding whether a page needs scrolling sideways. Every scan leaves out `nextjs-portal`, which is the development overlay. The list of public pages names the school's own address, `/schools/manchester/...`, rather than the Leeds one it redirects from. And each sweep is marked slow, because it opens twenty-odd screens and a development server compiles each one the first time it is asked for.
- **Options:** Leaving them and treating the failures as weather; stopping the bottom tab bar from spanning the scrollbar; waiting on `networkidle` before every scan.
- **Reason:** None of it was a fault in the product, and all of it had been failing on and off for weeks and being read as flakes, which is how a sweep stops being believed. A page long enough to scroll down gets a classic scrollbar in a headless browser, and anything fixed across the screen, which here is the bottom tab bar, is drawn to the width of the window rather than the width of the content; the sweep then reported exactly the scrollbar's nine pixels, on whichever screen happened to be long enough that run, which is why it moved about. Nothing there is content a person could scroll to. The crash that took the public pages out was the development overlay: it mounts and unmounts as the server compiles, axe walks frames, and a frame that goes mid-scan leaves it reading `documentElement` of nothing. That is why it landed on a different page every time and never in a build. The overlay is not our markup and is not in production, so it is excluded rather than waited for. The school page is a separate thing worth fixing anyway: the sweep was asking for an address that redirects, and `public-profile.spec.ts` already covers the redirect itself.

## D-220 | 2026-09-28 | A diary asks the server for itself again, rather than waiting on a websocket
- **Decision:** Every write made from the diary calls `router.refresh()` when it succeeds: booking a lesson and booking a weekly one, moving one, calling one off, dragging one into a gap, marking one done or a no-show, answering a request, and marking one paid or undoing that. `LiveDiary` stays exactly as it is, and goes back to being what it was for: changes made somewhere else, on another device or by a learner.
- **Options:** Leaving it as it was; waiting for the broadcast with a timeout and refreshing if it does not come; dropping the realtime channel and refreshing only.
- **Reason:** Nothing on the diary refreshed itself. A Server Action called `revalidatePath`, which marks the path stale on the server, and then the screen waited for the database to broadcast and `LiveDiary` to ask for the page again. When the channel had not joined yet, or a message was missed, the lesson was booked and the diary did not show it. `acceptance-01` had been failing about two runs in three on exactly that, and the booking was in the database every time; several specs assert `[data-diary-live="on"]` before they act, which is the same fault written down as a workaround. It matters more for the people using this than for the tests: an instructor between lessons on patchy signal taps Done, gets the toast, and watches nothing change. The realtime channel is kept because it is the only thing that catches a learner booking while the diary is open, but nothing a person does themselves should depend on it.

## D-221 | 2026-09-29 | The accessibility scan waits for the title the page streams
- **Decision:** `expectAccessible` waits up to five seconds for `document.title` to be something before it scans. A page that really has no title still fails, because the wait gives up quietly and axe then says so.
- **Options:** Leaving it and re-running; scanning the admin gallery before the change rather than after; giving the page a title in the shell rather than in `metadata`.
- **Reason:** `document-title (serious): Documents must have <title>` failed the admin gallery in CI, on a production build. A title arrives with the streamed metadata, after the shell, and a scan that starts in between sees a document without one; it is likelier after a Server Action, when the page is streaming itself again. It had been written down as a development-server race that "cannot happen in CI", and that was wrong, which is how it kept coming back on a different page each time. Nothing is weakened: the same assertion runs on the same list of violations, five seconds later at worst.

## D-222 | 2026-09-29 | Discard puts the edit away, history sits under the summary, and a pickup is changed rather than chosen again
- **Decision:** Three from the product owner's list. Discard, on the sheet asking whether the learner has been told about a move, closes the edit as well as the question; the cross and Escape still go back to the form, for somebody who wants a different time rather than no change at all. The learner card's History tab is gone and its list sits at the foot of the Summary tab. And a lesson that already has somewhere to start from shows a Change button beside Navigate rather than a list standing open: the list appears when Change is tapped, or by itself when there is nowhere to be collected from, and it carries one option that is not a place, "Add an address for [name]", which opens that learner's own pickup form with `?add=pickup`.
- **Options:** Leaving Discard as a way back to the form; keeping History as a tab; showing the list always; sending "add an address" to the learner card and letting them find the button.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for all three. Discard reads as throwing the change away, so leaving somebody on the form they were trying to leave is the wrong answer to the word; the two other ways out of that sheet still mean "not yet". History was a tab nobody opened, and a tab nobody opens hides what it holds; it is read after everything else about a person, which is where it now sits. And the pickup list standing open put a dropdown between the address an instructor came to read and the Navigate button they came to tap, for a change that is the rare case; the common case is reading it, so the common case is what the section shows. The add option is there because the moment somebody finds the place missing is the moment they want to add it, and making them find the learner card and then the button loses half of them on the way.

## D-223 | 2026-09-29 | An upload that fails says why
- **Decision:** The five storage helpers return where the picture went or why it did not, instead of a path or null, and all seven pickers show that sentence. A browser that could not reach the server at all gets a line about the connection; anything else carries what the server said, which is one of "Bucket not found", a policy refusal, or a size the bucket would not take.
- **Options:** Leaving the one message; logging the reason to the console; sending it to Sentry and saying nothing on screen.
- **Reason:** The product owner reported the gallery and the profile picture both refusing to upload on a phone, with "We could not upload that picture. Try again." There was no way to tell from that whether the phone could not reach the server, the bucket was not there yet, or a policy had refused the folder, and the local tests for both paths pass, so the message was the whole of the evidence and it said nothing. None of what the server says is secret, and the person reading it is the one who tried the upload. The console was not enough: nobody reporting a problem from a phone is reading a console.

## D-224 | 2026-09-29 | A picture is stored as what the browser could actually write
- **Decision:** The app asks a canvas whether it can write WebP before it asks for one, and falls back to PNG where it cannot. The object is named by what it turned out to be, `.webp` or `.png`, and the upload declares that same type. The five buckets accept `image/webp` and `image/png`, and the two that were capped at 2 MB take 5 MB, which is what a PNG of that picture comes to. PNG and not JPEG, deliberately.
- **Options:** Falling back to JPEG; leaving WebP alone and telling those phones to use another device; sniffing the blob's type after the fact and only fixing the declared type.
- **Reason:** Safari could not encode WebP from a canvas until 16.4, and a canvas asked for a type it cannot write quietly writes PNG instead, without an error. So an iPhone a year or two old produced a picture that was not what we then told the bucket it was, the bucket refused it, and the screen said "We could not upload that picture. Try again." That is how the product owner came to report both the gallery and the profile picture failing from a phone. The fallback is PNG rather than JPEG because of the rule the avatars bucket was built on: an untouched original cannot be stored even by a caller that skips the app, and that works only because no camera writes the type the bucket takes. A camera writes HEIC or JPEG; a canvas writes WebP or PNG. Allowing JPEG would have handed that rule away to save a few kilobytes on old phones, and what it protects is somebody's home address sitting in a public profile picture.

## D-225 | 2026-09-29 | Credit pays for what it covers, and the rest is paid the usual way
- **Decision:** Booking takes whatever credit the learner has with that Business, up to the length of the lesson, instead of taking it only when it covers the whole thing. An hour of credit against a two hour lesson leaves the lesson `unpaid` with `credit_minutes = 60`, and what is owed in money is the price less what those minutes were worth: `private.booking_owed_pence`, which every path now asks rather than reading `price_pence`. Cash, bank and card are all charged that amount. The pill says both ways it was paid, "Paid (credit + cash)", "Paid (credit + bank)", "Paid (credit + card)". Calling it off gives each part back the way it came, the minutes to the balance and the money as a refund. Twelve decision points across seven migrations that keyed off `payment_status = 'paid_credit'` now key off `credit_minutes > 0`.
- **And the fee is one fee, however it is paid for.** A lesson called off late or nobody came to costs a percentage of the price. Where credit paid for part of the lesson, the minutes the fee keeps pay what they are worth against it and `fee_pence` holds what is left, which is what the card is charged, what the cash sheet asks for and what the learner is told is owed. `cancellationOutcome` in `packages/core` works out the same figure for the screen before anybody presses the button. A lesson credit paid for in full now records a fee of nothing in money, where it used to record the whole price and never charge it.
- **Options:** Leaving credit all or nothing; letting credit cover the whole lesson by borrowing against the next package; charging the price and handing the credit's worth back as a refund; a switch per Business; keeping the gross fee in `fee_pence` and taking the credit off at each of the four places that ask for money.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it: "Upon new booking always use credit and charge the rest." An hour sitting in a learner's balance bought nothing towards a two hour lesson, so people who had paid for hours were asked for the full price of the next lesson and had to be talked through why. The value of the minutes comes from the lesson being booked, not from what the package cost, and it is rounded down so a learner is never handed value they did not buy; the penny of a lesson that does not divide stays with the Business, and the two halves always add back to the price, which is asserted at every split. Nothing is held in a wallet and no credit crosses a Business (PAY-12). The guard had to move off `paid_credit` because a part credit lesson is `unpaid` and then `paid_cash`, and every place that read the status would have dropped the minutes silently: cancelling would have kept the hour, a no-show would not have charged it, and moving one would have left the ledger pointing at a lesson it no longer matched. The fee had to move the same way, and for a sharper reason: a part credit lesson marked as a no-show kept the hour of credit as the fee and then asked for the whole fee in money as well, which on a two hour lesson at 82 pounds is 123 pounds for a lesson that costs 82. The net figure lives in the column rather than at the four places that ask for money, because those four would have had to agree with one another for ever, and the column is what all of them already read as what is owed.

## D-226 | 2026-09-29 | The app is painted on a canvas, and what sits on it is white
- **Decision:** A new colour token, `canvas` (#F5F5F7), is what the app is painted on: the portals and the admin console through `AppShell`, and signing in, the account screens and setting up through their layouts. `body` takes it too, so anything without a ground of its own lands on it. Cards, bars, sheets and the tiles that carry a figure are white. The public site keeps its white ground, which its own layout sets. And a second rule makes the first one work: the quiet fill. A secondary button, a chip, a toggle track or a tinted tile no longer names a grey, it asks what it is sitting on, through two custom properties the surfaces themselves set. On the canvas quiet is white and its hover is grey-100; on a white card quiet is grey-100 and its hover is grey-200. `bg-quiet` and `bg-quiet-strong` are the two utilities.
- **Two palette values moved with it, and they had to.** `blue` goes from #276EF1 to #2166E0 and `red` from #E11900 to #D81700. Both were a tenth of a point above the 4.5:1 that text has to clear on white, 4.58 and 4.84, so the moment the ground stopped being pure white they were under it: every link on a sign-in or an onboarding screen failed at 4.21, and a field's error message at 4.44. Twenty specs said so at once. Both are now 5.20 on white, 4.77 on the canvas and 4.68 on grey-100, and white on the new red reads better than it did on the old one. This is a departure from the PRD's 7.2 table, which names the two old values, and it is the product owner's to accept: the table is left as it is until they do.
- **Options:** Leaving everything white; making the cards grey on a white page; hand auditing all hundred `bg-grey-100` uses and hard coding each one; a `surface` prop on every control that could sit in either place; keeping grey controls on the canvas and accepting how they look.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, with iOS Settings as the reference: a grey ground with white cards on it. A card only reads as a card when the ground behind it differs, and the flat white app had nothing to separate one group from the next but hairlines. The quiet fill exists because grey-100 against the canvas is a contrast of 1.01, which is nothing: the first pass turned the controls on the canvas white by hand, and that broke the same controls inside cards, where white against white is just as invisible. Every chip on the money screen and the admin dates disappeared. Those are the same components in two places, so the answer could not be a colour written into them; it had to be a question they ask of whatever they are on, answered once by each surface and inherited. Hand auditing was the other way and it would have been wrong again the first time somebody moved a component. Nothing about the greys themselves changed, so a picture that has not loaded, a code block and the track behind a progress bar are all exactly as they were.

  **What this missed, and why (30 September).** The rule was right and the sweep was not: eleven screens were looked at and the components that name a grey were not. The product owner found four of them, the skill pills on a lesson record, the learner filters, everything below the photo on the profile, and a Sign out that read as a heading. Two were one line in `Chip`. A sweep of `packages/ui` then found nine more, including the rating buttons that sit one tap after the skill pills the owner reported. The lesson is that a change of ground is not checked by looking at screens, which shows only what happened to be built that way, but by reading every component that names a colour and asking which ground it assumed. On a white card all of them render exactly as before, because that is what the quiet fill resolves to there. The two colours moved because a palette has to work on the ground the app is painted on, and theirs had been pure white by accident rather than by choice: a contrast of 4.58 is not a decision anybody made, it is what was left after picking a blue that looked right.

## D-227 | 2026-09-29 | A database is emptied for a launch by a command that asks for the target twice
- **Decision:** `pnpm db:fresh` empties a database and leaves one super admin, for opening a project to the public (RUNBOOK 3.9). It removes every file in all five buckets through the storage API, truncates every table in `public` and `private` except six that hold reference data, deletes every account, and creates one super admin from `ADMIN_EMAIL` and `ADMIN_PASSWORD`. The list is the other way round from the seed's: everything goes unless it is named in `KEEP`, so a table added later holds somebody's data until somebody decides otherwise. It refuses unless `--project` names the same project the environment points at, and refuses again without `--yes`. The audit log and the credit ledger are append-only and refuse to be truncated at all; their guards are lifted for that one statement and put back, inside a transaction so a failure cannot leave them off.
- **Options:** A SQL script pasted into the dashboard; deleting the project and making a new one; `supabase db reset` against the hosted project; keeping the demo data and hiding it; naming the tables to empty rather than the tables to keep.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for a fresh start before the Phase 1 launch: no users, no records, no logs, the admin only. Deleting the project and starting again would have meant setting up the keys, the domain, the job runner and the payment provider from nothing, all of which are already right (RUNBOOK 3.1 to 3.8). `supabase db reset` re-runs the migrations, which is not the same thing and would have taken the reference data with it. The guard that matters is `--project`: this repository has already been bitten by production values sitting in `.env.local`, so a command that empties whatever it is pointed at is the wrong shape. Naming the project out loud, and having the command check that name against what it can see, is the difference between emptying staging and emptying the thing people are using. Keeping the tables to keep, rather than the tables to empty, is the same reasoning applied to the future: the failure mode of forgetting a new table is then an empty table rather than a stranger's data left in a public database.

## D-228 | 2026-09-30 | Nobody came, half an hour in | Product owner
- **Decision:** A no-show may be recorded thirty minutes after the lesson was due to start, where R-09 allowed fifteen, and the button appears at that moment rather than waiting for the lesson's booked time to be up. Marking a lesson done still waits for the end (D-213). The number lives in `noShowAfterMinutes` and in `mark_no_show`, and pgTAP now pins the boundary from both sides: twenty minutes in is refused, thirty-one is allowed.
- **Options:** Leaving it at fifteen; keeping both buttons behind the end of the lesson; making the wait a Business setting.
- **Reason:** The product owner's call, and both halves of it answer the same complaint. Fifteen minutes is a short wait to charge somebody the price of a lesson for, and half an hour is long enough that nobody records one on a whim; it is also about as long as an instructor will actually sit outside a house before giving up. The button not appearing until the booked hour was up was the other half: an instructor who waits half an hour and drives off to their next lesson had to remember to come back to it later, which is how a no-show quietly becomes an unpaid lesson nobody records. How a lesson went is still a question only once it is over, because that one has not happened yet.

## D-229 | 2026-09-30 | The Business's colour draws its public profile, and the profile says who checked the instructor | Product owner
- **Decision:** The colour a Business picks (D-210) now reaches `/instructors/<city>/<slug>`, where it draws four things: the edge of each pill under Next free times, the edge of the map, the edge of the prices card and the fill of the one Book a lesson button. The line under the name reads "Approved driving instructor, checked by Doovor" rather than "checked by us", with the name coming from `brand.ts`. `instructor_profile_page` carries `business.colour`, and changing the colour now expires every profile of that Business.
- **Options:** Leaving the colour on the booking link only; colouring the headings too; taking the colour from the instructor rather than the Business; leaving the cache to expire on its own.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it, and the profile is the page a learner is actually sent to: a booking link that looks like the Business and a profile that does not is the wrong way round. Four edges and one button is as far as it goes on purpose, because the page still has to read as the same product from one instructor to the next, and a heading in somebody's own colour is where that stops being true. Nothing about the colour's rules changed: a colour is not allowed to be somebody's own unless it clears 4.5:1 against white (D-210), which is what makes it safe behind the button's white label and more than the 3:1 an edge needs. The cache had to be dealt with in the same change rather than left: a profile is kept for hours, so without expiring it an instructor would pick a colour, look at their own page, and see the old one until it aged out, which is indistinguishable from the feature not working.

## D-230 | 2026-09-30 | One badge number, one account | Product owner
- **Decision:** A badge number belongs to one account. A unique index on `upper(btrim(badge_number))`, ignoring null and blank, is the guarantee; `submit_verification` checks the same thing first so the screen can say something useful, raising `BADGE_TAKEN`. Onboarding shows: "There is already an account with this badge number. If that is not you, email [support] with your badge number and photos of your badge." The address comes from `brand.ts`, which is why the sentence lives on the screen and not in the database.
- **Options:** Leaving it unchecked; checking only within a Business; warning rather than refusing; asking staff to spot it in the verification queue.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it. A badge number identifies one instructor to the DVSA, so two accounts holding the same one is either a typo or somebody teaching on a badge that is not theirs, and the second is the reason this is a refusal rather than a warning. Per Business would have been no check at all, since the case that matters is a stranger. Leaving it to the queue means staff catching by eye something the database can refuse for nothing, and only after the account exists. The sentence tells people what to do because both kinds of person read it: the one who mistyped, and the one whose badge somebody else has used, who needs a way to tell us.
- **What it caught immediately:** five places relied on duplicates. Four end to end specs typed the same badge number at both widths, which run at once, so each was two accounts with one badge; two pgTAP tests submitted `416234`, which is Sarah Khan's in the seed. Tests now take a badge number of their own from `aBadgeNumber()`.

## D-232 | 2026-09-30 | The Supabase CLI is pinned exactly, because 2.118.0 has no Windows binary
- **Decision:** `supabase` in the root `package.json` goes from `^2.117.0` to `2.117.0`, an exact pin. Dependabot's group bump to 2.118.0 is held until a release ships a Windows binary that installs.
- **Options:** Taking the bump and developing through WSL or Docker; excluding `supabase` from Dependabot altogether; leaving the caret and remembering not to install.
- **Reason:** 2.118.0 declares `@supabase/cli-windows-x64` as an optional dependency, and no stable 2.118.0 of that package exists on npm; nor does one for Linux, though CI got far enough to generate types, so it resolves there somehow. On Windows the CLI answers every command with "No matching Supabase CLI binary package found for win32-x64", which is every one of `db:reset`, `db:test`, `db:types`, `db:push` and the local stack: the whole of local development. The caret is what made it dangerous rather than merely broken. `^2.117.0` admits 2.118.0, so the version was never a decision anybody made: running `pnpm install` for an unrelated reason was enough to take it, which is how it was found. An exact pin makes the next move deliberate and visible in a diff.
- **What it means for the group bump:** pull request #9 bundles this with `next` and `eslint`, so it cannot be merged as it stands. The `next` part of it is already behind `main`, which is on 16.3.7 for the security fix (D-231 notes), so what is left of the group is an eslint patch.

## D-233 | 2026-10-01 | A paid lesson says how it was paid for, card included | Claude
- **Decision:** `lessonStateLabel` returns "Paid (card)" where it used to return nothing and let the pill fall back to its own word, "Paid". The method is now named on every pill that has one: "Paid (cash)", "Paid (bank)", "Paid (card)", and the three D-225 pairings where credit covered part of it. Credit on its own is untouched: that is a state rather than a method, so `lessonState` still calls it 'credit' and the pill says "Credit".
- **Options:** Leaving it as it was; "Paid (online)" instead of "Paid (card)"; naming no method at all and letting every paid lesson say "Paid".
- **Reason:** The product owner noticed it on a lesson they had just paid for by card and asked whether a bare "Paid" was deliberate. It was, in the sense that PAY-05 asks only that offline payments be told apart, so card was left to the default. It stopped reading that way once D-225 added "Paid (credit + card)": a learner who pays half on a card sees the card named, and one who pays all of it on a card does not. A bare word sitting among three qualified ones tells you only what it is not. "card" rather than "online" because the product already says it everywhere else: "Paid (credit + card)" on the pill, "Paid by card" on the receipt, "Card payments" on the setup screen.
- **Scope:** one line in `packages/core/src/diary.ts`. No migration and no stored value changes: `payment_status` already recorded `paid_card`, and nothing but the label was ever missing.

## D-234 | 2026-10-01 | Nothing about taking money is tested on staging until its own webhook exists
- **Decision:** A payments run on app.doovor.com needs three things set before it is worth starting: the four Stripe sandbox values in that deployment, `PAYMENTS_PROVIDER=stripe` beside them, and a hosted webhook endpoint in the sandbox pointed at `https://app.doovor.com/api/webhooks/stripe` with connected account events ticked. Until the endpoint exists, a card on staging will be taken by Stripe and the lesson will never be marked paid.
- **Options:** Testing on staging as it stands; forwarding staging's events through a local listener; leaving payments to local only until launch.
- **Order matters, because the app refuses to boot otherwise:** `env/schema.ts` requires all three of `NEXT_PUBLIC_STRIPE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY`, `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` and `STRIPE_CONNECT_WEBHOOK_SECRET` as soon as `PAYMENTS_PROVIDER` is `stripe`. Setting the provider before the endpoint exists does not degrade staging, it stops the build. So the endpoint is created first and all four values go in together.
- **Test mode is allowed there, and only because `APP_ENV` is still `preview`:** the live key guard reads `const live = env.APP_ENV === 'production'` and refuses a test key when that is true and a live key when it is false. app.doovor.com is a Vercel production deployment but carries `APP_ENV=preview` until launch (RUNBOOK 1), so test keys are not merely permitted there, they are the only ones that will boot. The corollary is that flipping `APP_ENV` to `production` at launch invalidates these four values in the same breath, which is what makes RUNBOOK 3.7 step 9 a single simultaneous swap rather than a sequence.
- **Reason:** Pro is granted and a lesson is marked paid only by a signed webhook event, never by the browser coming back from Stripe, which is what makes the amount unforgeable (owner's requirement, 9.18). The consequence is that an environment Stripe cannot reach cannot complete a payment, however correct the keys are. The local run worked because `stripe:listen` was standing in for that endpoint. Forwarding staging's events through a laptop was rejected as a way to test something that has to work when the laptop is off. The connected account made locally does carry over, because staging uses the same sandbox.

## D-235 | 2026-10-01 | A subscription event is applied exactly once, structurally | Claude
- **Decision:** Stripe Billing events go through `system_process_billing_event`, which records the event in `provider_events` and applies it in the same transaction, the way payment events already do. `service_role` has no execute grant on `system_record_subscription` or `system_record_subscription_payment`, so the wrapper is the only way in.
- **Options:** Calling the two functions straight from the webhook; checking for a repeat in TypeScript; making the payment function idempotent on its own.
- **Reason:** Stripe delivers an event at least once and sometimes three times. One of those two functions adds to `months_paid` and marks referral months spent, so a second delivery would hand somebody a loyalty discount they had not earned and spend a referral month on an invoice that had already been paid for. Checking in TypeScript would have left the check and the write in different transactions, which is the bug one layer down. Taking the grants away makes it structural: there is no call a webhook handler could make that skips the record, so this cannot be got wrong later by somebody adding a second handler.
- **Also:** how many months an invoice covers is worked out in SQL from the interval on the row, not taken from the event. The caller cannot say "this invoice was worth twelve months".

## D-236 | 2026-10-01 | A Server Action uses the secret key for one `system_*` call | Claude
- **Decision:** `startProCheckout` calls `system_start_subscription` with the secret key, after deriving the Business from the session. This is a third narrow exception to the rule that Server Actions use the user's session client, alongside D-068 and D-158.
- **Options:** Granting the function to `authenticated`; a new `authenticated` function that derives the Business from `auth.uid()`; writing no row until the webhook arrives.
- **Reason:** The row holds the Stripe customer id, and the customer id is the thing `system_record_subscription` matches a webhook against. A function `authenticated` could call would have to take that id from its caller, and a caller who passed somebody else's customer id would point their own Business at a subscription somebody else is paying for. That is the escalation this is avoiding, and it is worse than the exception. Writing nothing until the webhook would mean trusting event metadata to name the Business, which the existing function refuses to do on purpose: "inventing a Business from it would be guessing whose money it is."
- **Why it is narrow:** no tenant data is read with the secret key, the Business comes from `requireAccess()` and never from the request body, and the row it writes is `incomplete`, which carries no Pro. The grant on the function is to `service_role` only.

## D-237 | 2026-10-01 | A renewal notice may be a text, which NTF-01 did not allow | Claude
- **Decision:** `subscription.renewing` joins `booking.reminder` as a kind that may use SMS. A yearly renewal is warned about 14 days ahead by text as well as email; a monthly one 3 days ahead by email only. The catalogue test that pinned SMS to one kind now pins it to two, with the reason beside it.
- **Options:** Email only, as NTF-01 reads; texting both intervals; leaving the rule alone and sending the text some other way.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for texts on renewals. NTF-01 says "SMS for reminders only", and this is a reminder of the same sort: a thing about to happen that somebody may want to stop first. The difference is what happens if they do not read it, which here is £120 leaving an account by itself. A monthly renewal is £12 and three days is enough, so it does not get a text: the allowance is finite and the point is proportion, not reach.
- **Scope:** this is a deviation from a P1 requirement's wording and is flagged in the milestone report. The amount in the notice is the discounted one, worked out the same way the charge will be, so somebody on 30% off is warned about £84 rather than £120.
- **One notice per renewal:** the day it renews is in the notification's dedupe key, so a daily job inside a fortnight's window writes one row, not fourteen. That matters more than usual because a duplicate here costs money.

## D-238 | 2026-10-01 | The loyalty discount has to reach Stripe, not just the screen | Claude
- **Decision:** When an invoice is recorded, `system_process_billing_event` answers with the run of months before it and after it, and the webhook handler works out what each is worth and asks Stripe for the new price when they differ. Nothing is prorated.
- **Options:** Leaving it as it was; a nightly job that reprices everybody; working the price out in SQL; repricing on every invoice whether or not the step moved.
- **Reason:** A Stripe subscription charges whatever it was created with until something changes it, and nothing did. So somebody who subscribed in their first month would have gone on paying £12 for ever while the plan screen promised that "every 3 months takes another 5% off". The promise was true of a new subscription and of nothing else, which is worse than not making it. A nightly job would have been a second place that decides prices; doing it on the invoice means the new price is set the moment the old one was last charged.
- **Why the months come back rather than the price going down:** the prices live in `plans.ts` and the database has none in it, which is the whole reason no amount can be forged. Counting months stays in SQL, where only a signed event moves it; pricing stays in TypeScript, where the plan is. Repricing only when the figure actually changes keeps Stripe from collecting a price object per invoice.
- **It may fail, and that is allowed:** the payment is already recorded and the plan already granted when this runs, so a price that could not be moved is logged as nothing and moves on the next invoice. Answering Stripe anything but 200 would have it send the whole event again.

## D-239 | 2026-10-01 | A second subscription is cancelled and refunded | Product owner
- **Decision:** A Business can have one subscription. If a second live one arrives for the same customer, `system_process_billing_event` refuses to record it and hands it back to the app, which cancels it at Stripe immediately and refunds the invoice with Stripe's own `duplicate` reason. Any customer balance the duplicate spent, which is referral months, goes back on. What came back is written to the audit trail as `subscription.refunded`.
- **Options:** Leaving it; cancelling the duplicate without a refund; letting support handle it by hand; locking the checkout so a second one cannot start.
- **Reason:** The product owner chose refund. The window is real but small: two browser tabs can both reach Stripe's page and both finish in the seconds between the first checkout and the event that grants Pro. What made it worth closing is what used to happen next, which was not "two subscriptions" but something worse: the row is one per Business, so the later event wrote its own subscription id over the first, and the first went on billing a card every month with nothing in our database pointing at it. Nobody would have found it except the person being charged.
- **Which one is the duplicate:** whatever the row already holds and is carrying Pro is kept, and anything else arriving for the same customer is the duplicate. That rule needs no clock and no ordering, which matters because webhook deliveries have neither. An update to the one being kept is not a duplicate however often it arrives, and a new subscription for a Business whose last one ended is somebody coming back rather than a duplicate: both are covered by pgTAP.
- **Why not a lock on the checkout:** a lock would have to be held across a redirect to a page we do not control and back, and a lock somebody abandons by closing the tab is a Business that cannot subscribe until it expires. Refusing at the point the truth is known is simpler and cannot strand anybody.
- **It is allowed to fail:** the refund runs after the event is recorded, and the webhook answers 200 whatever happens, because anything else would have Stripe send the event again and ask for a second refund. A refund that could not be made leaves the audit row saying it was asked for, not that it happened.

## D-240 | 2026-10-01 | basic-ftp is raised a whole major to clear a CPU advisory | Claude
- **Decision:** `basic-ftp` is overridden to `>=6.2.1 <7`. It resolved to 5.3.1, and the fix for GHSA-c475-qrg2-pj4r landed only in 6.2.1, so clearing it means taking a major bump of a transitive dependency.
- **Options:** Adding the advisory to `ignoreGhsas` beside the other two; pinning Lighthouse CI back; taking the major.
- **Reason:** A quadratic-time denial of service in the Unix directory listing parser of `Client.list()`, which turned CI red on a push that had nothing to do with it. It reaches us three ways, all under `@lhci/cli`, through the proxy resolver and the browser downloader. Neither path runs here: the Lighthouse job measures with the runner's own Chrome and resolves no `ftp://` URI. Ignoring it would have been defensible on that ground, but `ignoreGhsas` in this repo is for advisories with **no fixed version**, which is why `extract-zip` is in there and this is not. The workspace file already says why: "we do not use that path" is a statement about this week's configuration, not a property of the dependency.
- **Bounded at both ends**, like every override beside it. That convention exists because an open `>=1.1.20` on brace-expansion was satisfied by 5.0.9 and lifted minimatch 3 four majors while leaving it vulnerable anyway.
- **What to watch:** this is a major bump for a package Lighthouse CI's `get-uri` depends on, so the risk is that Lighthouse breaks rather than that anything of ours does. The Lighthouse job is what proves it, and it is green.

## D-241 | 2026-10-01 | A report has a reference, reaches people from anywhere, and is answered twice | Product owner
- **Decision:** Five changes to "tell us something" (D-202). Pictures show as thumbnails with a tile while they upload, instead of the words "Picture 1" and a cross. A support button sits in every portal header, to the left of the bell, opening the same form in a sheet. Every report gets a reference. The sender is emailed when it arrives and again when a person deals with it.
- **Options considered on the reference:** a second form of the uuid; counting rows; a sequence.
- **Reason for a sequence:** "R01 and so on" needs to be short enough to read down a phone, so a uuid was out. Counting rows would hand two reports arriving in the same second the same number, and the whole point of a reference is that it is one person's. A sequence cannot do that. The format is "R" and the number padded to two digits, so R01 through R99 look exactly as asked and R100 follows without anything special happening: a two-digit field would have run out at the hundredth report, which this product expects to pass.
- **Why the emails go through the outbox:** `submit_feedback` and `admin_handle_feedback` enqueue an event in the same transaction as the row they write, so a confirmation is never promised for a report that rolled back and never missed for one that did not. The action does not flush the outbox itself, which would have been the only write in the app that did: the sweep runs every minute, and the reference is on the screen immediately either way.
- **Only on the way in:** dealing with a report twice emails once, and putting one back emails nobody. Staff putting a report back is them correcting themselves, and "your report is no longer dealt with" is not news anybody needs. Dealing with it again after that is news again, and does send.
- **Thumbnails without a round trip:** the bucket is private, so a thumbnail cannot be the stored path. The blob that was uploaded is kept as an object URL in the tab and shown, and revoked when the picture is taken off or the form goes. Taking a picture off now also deletes it from the bucket, which it did not before: a screenshot left in private storage attached to nothing is a thing nobody could later find to delete.
- **Found while doing it:** the file input was never cleared after choosing, so picking the same file again after a failure fired no change event and looked broken. It is cleared now.
- **A trap worth writing down:** `Field` gives its one child the id its label points at. Wrapping the file input in a div to hold the thumbnails under it moved that id onto the div and left the input unlabelled, which the accessibility sweep caught as critical. The thumbnails sit outside the `Field` instead.

## D-242 | 2026-10-12 | Pro cannot be paid for until its webhook can be received | Product owner
- **Decision:** Paying for Pro is open only where the event that grants it can arrive: with the fake, always; with Stripe, only once `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` is set. Until then the plan screen offers no checkout, and `startProCheckout` refuses even if called directly.
- **Options:** A feature flag to switch it on; leaving it open; hiding the card without refusing in the action.
- **Reason:** The product owner wants the first instructors on before Pro billing is finished, which works because every one of them starts on Pro through a founding place or a trial. But production needs live Stripe keys for card payments between learners and instructors, and the same keys could take a Pro payment. Pro is granted by a signed webhook and never by the redirect back from Stripe, so a checkout without the platform endpoint would take real money and record nothing: the card charged, Stripe retrying an event we refuse for three days, the plan unchanged. Keying it to the secret rather than a flag means there is no switch to forget: the endpoint existing is what opens it. Refusing in the action as well as hiding the card matters because a hidden button can still be pressed by anybody who knows what it posts.

## D-243 | 2026-10-12 | The founding counter is the database's count and cannot be anything else | Product owner, wording; Claude, the source
- **Decision:** The home page and the pricing page show how many of the first hundred instructor places have gone, as "46/100 joined" with a bar and the places left, read from the Businesses that hold one through `public.founding_places()`. Before anybody has joined it says "100 places open"; once all have gone the band stops offering a place and says new businesses still start free.
- **Options:** A fixed number in the page; a number staff type into the admin screen; the database's own count.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for the line to read "46/100 joined for free 12 months". 46 was the example, and nobody has joined yet. A number on a public page about how scarce an offer is, is a claim to the public: under the Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act 2024 a false one is a misleading action, and fake scarcity is one of the things the CMA pursues by name. A typed number is true on the day it is typed at best. The count of places given is true by construction, and it is the same rows the places are given out from, so the page cannot run ahead of the offer. It is kept for minutes so the home page stays a page built once, and a count that cannot be read leaves the counter off rather than guessing.
- **The closed state matters as much:** an offer printed after it has closed is the other way for this number to lie, so the band changes with it. It does not name a number of trial days, because the trial is 90 days for the next 500 and 30 after, and a figure there would be the next thing to go stale.

## D-244 | 2026-10-12 | Logo files go in assets/brand, as masters, and everything else is made from them | Claude
- **Decision:** A folder at the repository root, `assets/brand`, holds the logo as the designer sends it: horizontal, horizontal in white, stacked, icon, an app icon on its background, and an optional tagline lockup, all SVG. Every favicon, app icon, email header and share image is made from those, rather than exported by hand. The slogan "Drive Starts Here" is recorded in brand.ts.
- **Options:** Asking for every size as a separate export; putting the files straight into the app's public folder.
- **Reason:** Twenty exports by hand is twenty chances for one to be a pixel off or a version behind, and the app already draws its own icons in code, so feeding it the master is less work and stays right when the logo changes. The folder is outside the app and outside the copy guard on purpose: a designer's SVG often carries the brand name in its title or ids, which the guard would fail the build on, and a raw export should not be served as it is. What the app serves is made from it, optimised and stripped.

## D-245 | 2026-10-12 | Four advisories in eleven days, one of them in production | Claude
- **Decision:** `next` to 16.3.8 with a tilde, `sharp` to `>=0.35.5 <0.36`, `source-map-js` to `>=1.2.2 <2`, and `braces` GHSA-vfj7-8cjw-p6xm added to `ignoreGhsas`.
- **Why now:** CI's audit step went red on a push that changed none of these, eleven days after the last green one. Every other job passed.
- **The one that matters:** GHSA-cjq9-62q9-8jv4 is a server-side request forgery in Next's image optimisation, which every page with a picture goes through, on the production server. `sharp` (GHSA-wq5f-xc86-pv6w) is what that optimisation resizes with, and its librsvg can be made to misbehave by a crafted SVG, the week the logo is about to arrive as SVG. These two are a visitor's request reaching a weakness, not a developer's machine.
- **A tilde on next, not a caret:** `^16.3.8` resolves to 16.4.0, a minor released six days later. A security fix should be the release that fixes it and nothing else, so `~16.3.8` keeps it to the 16.3 line. 16.4.0 comes through Dependabot on its own, as a change to read.
- **Why braces is ignored rather than raised:** there is no fixed version: 3.0.3 is the last release and is listed as vulnerable. That is the one case `ignoreGhsas` is for, which is why `basic-ftp` (D-240) was raised and this is not. It reaches us only through ESLint's Next plugin finding our own files, so the patterns it is given are the repository's globs.

## D-246 | 2026-10-12 | The launch wipe restarts every number, so the founder is D000001 | Claude
- **Decision:** The two sequences made on their own, `platform_number_seq` (the D number) and `feedback_reference_seq` (the R number), are owned by the columns they number. A pgTAP test fails if any sequence in `public` or `private` is not owned by a column.
- **Found by:** reading `pnpm db:fresh` before the product owner ran it for real. It truncates with `RESTART IDENTITY`, which restarts a sequence only if a column of a truncated table owns it. Neither of these was owned, so the truncate went straight past them: the new super admin would have been D0000 and whatever the demo accounts reached, not the D000001 D-176 promised the founder, and the first real report would have carried on from the demo ones rather than being R01.
- **Options:** Resetting the two sequences by name inside `db:fresh`; owning them by their columns.
- **Reason:** Resetting by name is a list somebody has to remember to add the next one to. Ownership is a property of the sequence, so the truncate restarts it with no list at all, and the test makes forgetting loud: it fails the day a standalone sequence is added, not the day after a launch. Nothing changes in ordinary running, because nothing truncates either table, so a D number is still never given to anybody else. A launch wipe is the one moment it should start again, since everybody it was promised to goes with it.
- **Proved by rehearsal:** the wipe was run against the local stack. The admin came out D000001, no Businesses were left, the founding counter read 0 of 100, the next report was R01, and the cities and settings were kept.

## D-247 | 2026-10-12 | The launch wipe reads a pasted connection string by its shape | Claude
- **Decision:** `pnpm db:fresh` reads `SUPABASE_DB_URL` with `databaseUrlFrom`, which takes the password to be everything between the first `:` after the user and the last `@` before the host, and puts the string back together with the user and password encoded properly. Anything actually wrong is said in words, naming the variable and never repeating its value.
- **Found by:** the product owner running the wipe for real. It stopped at once with "Invalid URL" and nothing else. Nothing had been touched, because the URL is checked before anything is deleted, but nothing said which of the two addresses it meant or why.
- **Why it happened:** Supabase's Connect panel shows the string with `[YOUR-PASSWORD]` where the password goes, and the password goes in as it is. A `#`, `/` or `?` in a password ends the part of an address that holds it, so a URL parser reads a host and a port out of the middle of the password and gives up. That is ordinary for a generated password, and nobody should have to percent-encode one by hand to empty a database.
- **Options:** Telling people to percent-encode the password; asking for the password separately and building the string from pieces; reading the pasted string by its shape.
- **Reason:** The pasted string is what Supabase gives people, so it is what the command should take. Encoding by hand is exactly the step that goes wrong at the moment it matters most. A password somebody did encode comes out the same, because it is decoded first. A password typed inside the placeholder's square brackets has them taken off, and the run says so. The placeholder left in is refused by name.
- **Proved without risk:** run with a hosted-looking string and without `--yes`, which refuses before anything connects. The placeholder is refused in words; a password with `#`, `/` and `?` now passes to the `--yes` check where it used to stop.

## D-248 | 2026-10-12 | Every key the environment guard reads reaches the build | Claude
- **Decision:** `SUPABASE_SEND_SMS_HOOK_SECRET` joins `globalPassThroughEnv` in `turbo.json`, and `apps/web/src/env/turbo.test.ts` fails if any key in the server or client schema is not named there.
- **Found by:** the first production build after `APP_ENV` became `production` in Vercel. It failed with "SUPABASE_SEND_SMS_HOOK_SECRET is required when this is production", although Vercel has held the value since 20 September and sign-in codes arrive. A failed build is never promoted, so app.doovor.com kept serving the one before it.
- **Why it happened:** Turbo runs the build with only the variables `turbo.json` names and drops the rest without a word. The guard is checked while the pages are built, so it saw nothing. At run time Vercel gives the functions every variable, which is why the hook itself has always had its secret. D-192 added the requirement and the variable but not the pass-through, and no build before this one had `APP_ENV` set to `production`, so nothing asked.
- **Options:** Name the key; pass `SUPABASE_*` through; turn off Turbo's strict environment mode.
- **Reason:** Naming it matches how every other secret is listed. A wildcard or the loose mode would hand the build variables it has no use for. The test makes the next missed key fail in CI rather than on launch day.

## D-249 | 2026-10-12 | The logo is drawn from its masters' shapes, everywhere it shows | Claude
- **Decision:** `pnpm brand:logo` reads the SVG masters in `assets/brand` into plain shape data in `packages/config/src/logo`, and everything is drawn from that: a `Logo` component in `packages/ui` for the site's header and footer, the app's top bar and menu, sign-in, account and onboarding; the app icons, `/favicon.ico` (16, 32 and 48 px), `/favicon.svg`, the share images and `/brand/email-logo.png` through the image renderer; and the offline page as SVG text. A test fails if the data drifts from the masters, the white files must be the same artwork as the black ones, and an export that breaks a rule is refused by name with nothing written.
- **Options:** Serving the exported files from the public folder; inlining the SVG text from the files at build time; drawing from shape data made from them.
- **Reason:** Served as files, each colour needs its own export and its own request, a raw export carries editing leftovers and ids that clash when two sit on one page, and the copy guard would have to look away from the public folder. Drawn from shapes, one component paints the logo in the text colour, so black on light and white on dark are the same thing, and the image renderer and the offline page use the same shapes. Each logo is its own module, so a page that shows one carries only that one.
- **The email logo is a picture on the email's own grey,** not a transparent one: email cannot show SVG, and a mail app that darkens the background would leave a transparent black logo invisible. With pictures turned off the name shows in its place, styled as the old wordmark was. The plain text version no longer starts with the name; the sender and the footer still say who it is from.
- **The share images use the horizontal logo,** not the slogan lockup the README first suggested: a preview is shown at about a third of its size, where the slogan is a smudge.
- **The maskable app icon draws the master at 80%,** putting the wheel at 62% of the square, so it keeps a ring of black inside the circle Android always keeps. The ordinary icons use the master as drawn, with the wheel at 77%.
- **The white horizontal file draws exactly as the black one** (no pixel differs at 4096 px wide) but writes its paths differently, so the check compares the frame, the shapes and their fill rules, not the path text.

## D-250 | 2026-10-12 | The tab icon is round, from its own master, and the logo is bigger | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** The product owner added `assets/brand/favicon-icon.svg`, the white wheel on a black circle, for the browser tab, because the square app icon did not look good there. `/favicon.ico` and `/favicon.svg` are drawn from it with the corners clear, and the page no longer offers the square 192 px app icon as a tab icon: the square icons are for installing, in the manifest. The logo reader now knows a backdrop, a square or a circle filling the frame behind the shapes, and refuses any other square or circle.
- **The logo is bigger,** as the product owner asked: twice the size in the site footer (64 px), and a little bigger in every header (40 px on the site, sign-in, account and onboarding, 32 px in the app's top bar and menu). The footer's first column is wider on a large screen so the logo fits it.
- **The founding count is on the instructors' and the schools' pages too,** which showed the offer without it. Each counts the places of the people it is talking to: instructors on the page for instructors, driving schools on the page for schools.

## D-251 | 2026-10-12 | The instructors' page sells the product feature by feature, and founding prices show as £0 | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** `/instructors-software` is rebuilt to convert: the founding offer and its real count over the title, "Less admin. More lessons. Paid on time.", then six features each told beside a glimpse of the app (bookings, payments, the day, lesson records, getting found, money and tax), what else is included, the set up steps, the founding offer, the plans, the questions instructors ask, and a closing call. Every feature named is built; what is on Pro says "On Pro"; what is coming stays on the plans, marked as coming (D-116).
- **Founding prices:** wherever a plan card shows (the pricing page, the page for instructors, the page for schools), a paid plan with founding places left shows £0 beside its own price struck through, "Free for 1 year", what it costs after ("then £12 per month, or £120 a year"), and the places left from the database. Pro counts the instructors' places and School the schools'. When the places have gone, or cannot be counted, the card shows the plan's price as it is.
- **"1 year", not "12 months":** the product owner asked for the offer to read as free for a year, so the founding term is said in years when it is whole years, on the founding band, the plans and the questions, from one helper in `@repo/core/founding`.
- **Options:** A longer grid of features; a page per feature; the story layout with the app's own glimpses.
- **Reason:** A visitor deciding whether to switch needs to see the thing doing their job, one job at a time, and the glimpses are the app's own components, so they are true to it and cost no images. The struck price is the price a founder pays when the year ends and everybody pays once the places have gone, so the comparison is a true one, as the CMA's guidance on price comparisons asks; the places left are the database's own count (D-243), never a number written in.
- **Not on the page:** reviews, pass rates, mock tests and test readiness, which are in the PRD for a later phase and not built. No testimonials until there are real ones (CLAUDE.md rule 7).

## D-252 | 2026-10-12 | The PRD says what the product is, and its change log is folded in | Product owner
- **Decision:** The PRD describes the product as it is, without saying what was added or changed. Every row of its section 21, "What changed while it was built", is written into the requirement or rule it concerned, and the section is gone; so are the sentences elsewhere that told the history rather than the rule. Decision numbers stay beside the requirements they shaped, because they are how a requirement is traced, not a record of change. The decisions since the log was last kept (D-233 to D-251) are written in the same way, and the menus, the open decisions and the release plan say where things stand on 12 October 2026, version 1.2.
- **Options:** Keeping the change log and adding to it; a separate document of changes; one PRD that reads as the current product.
- **Reason:** The product owner asked for it. A reader wants to know what the product does, and two places saying it, the requirement and a log that amends it, is how they disagree: BOK-08 still let a learner move a lesson while the log said only the instructor could. What changed and why is already kept, with the options, in this file and in `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

## D-253 | 2026-10-13 | The slogan is written with its full stop | Claude, on the product owner's word
- **Decision:** "Drive Starts Here." everywhere, with the full stop the logo's lockup draws, in `brand.ts` and the PRD.
- **Options:** With the full stop; without it, leaving the artwork and the text to differ.
- **Reason:** The product owner left it to us. The artwork is the designer's and is not ours to change, so the text follows it and the slogan reads the same wherever it appears. It stands on its own line wherever it is used, so the full stop never lands mid-sentence.

## D-254 | 2026-10-13 | A car's first 10,000 business miles are 55p each from the 2026 to 2027 tax year | Claude
- **Decision:** `amapRates` takes the rate of the tax year a claim is for: 45p and 25p from 2011 to 2012, and 55p and 25p from 2026 to 2027, as GOV.UK's table of simplified expenses for vehicles lists them. A year HMRC has not set carries on at the latest rates.
- **Found by:** checking the facts for the guide to instructors' expenses against GOV.UK. The module had 45p for every year, so the books for 2026 to 2027 claimed £1,000 too little on the first 10,000 miles.
- **Options:** One rate for every year, changed in place; a rate per tax year from the year it starts.
- **Reason:** A rate changed in place would rewrite every earlier year's claim along with it, and HMRC still asks for 45p for miles before 6 April 2026. The tests now state each year's rate, which is what made the old assumption visible.
- **Then:** GOV.UK names "dual control driving instructors' cars" among the cars that cannot use the flat rates at all, and the mileage log did not ask whether a car has dual controls. Raised with the product owner, who asked for it to be fixed: D-256.

## D-255 | 2026-10-13 | Guides are written as typed data in the code, in a small Markdown, by the team | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** The site has guides at `/guides`, to rank for what instructors and learners search for. Each is a typed object in `packages/core/src/guides/library`, its body in a small Markdown that `parseGuideMarkdown` reads into blocks (headings, paragraphs, both lists, tables, a note; bold and links inside a line) and refuses anything else by name, HTML included. The site draws them in its own components. Every guide is by "The [brand] team" (the product owner's choice), says when it was last checked, lists the GOV.UK pages its facts come from, and is given to search as an Article with its questions as an FAQ and breadcrumbs. Guides have a sitemap of their own, `/sitemaps/guides.xml`, in the index; a share image each; and a link in the site's menu. A test holds every guide to the rules: a description search will not cut off, at least three sections and three questions, sources on GOV.UK, links only to pages that exist.
- **Options:** A blog on a separate platform or subdomain; a paid headless CMS; Markdown files read at build; typed data in the code.
- **Reason:** Guides on the site's own domain lend their ranking to every other page. Typed data needs no new dependency or supplier, is checked by the same tests, lint and copy guard as everything else (so the brand is interpolated and no long dash gets in), and can quote the app's own numbers: the expenses guide reads the mileage rates from the module the books use. A Markdown file in `apps/` would have tripped the copy guard on the brand name. An editor in `/admin` remains ADM-09, Phase 2, if the product owner wants to write without a developer.
- **The first three:** Making Tax Digital for driving instructors, driving instructor expenses, and the 2026 driving test booking rules, each checked against GOV.UK on 13 October 2026.

## D-256 | 2026-10-13 | A car with dual controls is claimed on what it costs, and the books ask | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** Adding a car asks "Does it have dual controls?", Yes or No, with no answer chosen for the instructor. A car with dual controls is settled on its actual costs the moment it is added (`add_vehicle` with `p_dual_controls`, audited as `vehicle.method_set` because of `dual_controls`), so the mileage log never offers it and `record_mileage` refuses it as it refuses any car claimed on costs. `vehicles.dual_controls` is kept, and a check on the table refuses a dual control car with any other method, so even a write that went round the functions could not claim one by the mile. The cars list says "Dual controls"; the mileage screen explains, when every car is claimed on costs, that HMRC does not allow the rate for them and points to expenses.
- **Options:** Ask the question and settle the method (chosen); warn beside the mileage log and leave the choice to the instructor; drop the mileage log.
- **Reason:** GOV.UK lists dual control driving instructors' cars among the vehicles that cannot use the flat rates, and most instructors teach in one, so a mileage claim for one would be wrong on the return. Settling the method when the car is added uses the rule the books already enforce (one method per car, for its life) rather than a second one. A car without dual controls, which some instructors use, keeps the mileage rate. The production database had no cars, so nothing needed converting. The instructors' page no longer shows a mileage claim in its picture of the tax year: it shows a dual control car's costs, as most instructors will see.

## D-258 | 2026-10-14 | Every email signs off the same way, and Auth's emails are drawn from the same layout | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** The layout every email shares ends with why it came, "Need help? Email us at" the support address, the logo again (smaller) with the slogan, links to the privacy notice, both sets of terms and the site, and "[brand] is owned and run by" the company disclosure that brand.ts already gives the site's footer, with the ICO registration. The four emails Supabase Auth sends itself are now React Email components in `packages/emails/src/auth.tsx`, in the same layout, each with a black button and, under it, "If the button does not work, copy this link into your browser:" and the link. They are rendered once, with Auth's own placeholders, into `supabase/templates`, which the local stack reads and a hosted push copies into `ops/staging`. A test renders them again and fails when a template has fallen behind; others check the button is the first link, the subjects match both configs, and the "works for 1 hour" line matches `otp_expiry`.
- **Copy guard:** the generated templates carry the brand name, domain and colours because brand.ts put them there, so the guard leaves `supabase/templates/*.html` out of its brand rule. Dashes are still refused there, and a file that only resembles one is checked as before.
- **Options:** Hand-written HTML in the dashboard; a send-email hook so the app sends Auth's emails itself through Resend; templates rendered from the shared components (chosen).
- **Reason:** One source for every email, so the confirmation looks like the receipts and notices and cannot drift from them. A hook would add an endpoint, a secret and a dependency on the app being up for anybody to sign in, for the same result. The hosted project only changes when its settings are pushed or pasted, which the runbook now says how to do.
- **Found on the way:** React adds a preload for the logo to an email's head, which is an `href` before the button's, so the end to end mail helper now follows the first link to tap (`<a href>`).

## D-259 | 2026-10-14 | Somebody is welcomed when their email is first confirmed | Product owner, Claude
- **Decision:** A new notification, `account.welcome`, by email and in the inbox, essential, to learners, instructors and schools: "Welcome to [brand]", what is next for the side they signed up for, and "Any questions as you get going? Email" the support address. Its button goes to `/start`, which sends each of them on to onboarding or their portal. The auth triggers queue `account/email-confirmed` when an address is confirmed for the first time (the confirmation link, or a first sign-in link to an unconfirmed address), and when somebody signs up with Google or Apple, which confirm the address themselves. A job reads the name and side through `system_welcome_notice` and writes the notification once, by its dedupe key; the dispatcher emails it.
- **Not welcomed:** an account made with its address already confirmed by email, which is a seed, staff or a fresh start (D-246), and a learner an instructor added by hand (D-068) until they confirm their own address. A changed address confirmed later is not a new account.
- **Options:** Send from the `/auth/confirm` route (it cannot tell a confirmation from a sign-in link, both arrive as `type=email`, and misses Google); send at the end of onboarding (late, and a learner may stop before then); queue it where the database sees the address confirmed (chosen).
- **Reason:** The confirmation is the moment the product owner named, and the database is the one place that sees it whichever way it happens. Going through the notification pipeline gives the branded email and footer (D-258), the inbox record and the one-per-person guarantee without a new sender.

## D-260 | 2026-10-14 | An instructor, and their school, hear how a badge was decided and when it is running out | Claude
- **Found:** while adding the email the product owner asked for when staff verify an instructor. `decide_verification` queued `instructor/verification-decided` and the daily sweep sent `instructor/badge-expiring`, but no job listened to either, so Appendix B's "Verification approved or rejected" and "Badge expiry" had never reached anybody, by email or otherwise.
- **Decision:** Two jobs now listen. Both read the instructor through `system_instructor_notice`: their name, how and why the badge was last decided, when it runs out, how many times it has been decided, and the active owners and managers of the school they teach for, if any. The instructor hears "You are verified", or "Your badge was not verified" with the reason and "Send it again from your profile and we will check it"; a school hears "[name] is verified" or "[name] was not verified". A badge running out is the sweep's own words to the instructor ("Renew it with the DVSA, then send the new one from your profile") and the day it runs out to the school. The buttons open the instructor's profile, or the school's instructors.
- **Once each:** a decision's notice is keyed by how many times that badge has been decided, counted from the append-only audit log, so a second refusal is a second notice and a retried event is not. A warning is keyed by the badge's expiry and the days before it, so a renewed badge is warned about again.
- **Options:** Write the notifications inside `decide_verification` (it would need rebuilding from its newest definition, and every other notice is written by a job after the change, D-017); a job per event reading through one lookup (chosen).
- **Reason:** The events were already queued with the right identifiers; what was missing was the listener, and the lookup is the same for both.

## D-261 | 2026-10-14 | The sitemap index lists a type only once it has a page | Claude
- **Found:** in Google Search Console, at the product owner's request. The index at `/sitemap.xml` was read successfully, but the instructors, schools and places sitemaps each showed "1 error": "Missing XML tag", parent `urlset`, tag `url`. Production has nobody listed yet, so those sitemaps had no `<url>` in them, which Google reports as an error.
- **Decision:** `sitemapKindsWithPages` keeps the kinds with at least one page, and the index lists only those. The site's own pages and the guides are always there; instructors, schools and places join the index with their first page, when the entries are read fresh after a profile changes (D-114). Each kind's own sitemap still answers at its address.
- **Options:** Leave the empty sitemaps and the errors (they would clear on their own once something is listed); put a placeholder page in each (a page that is not a profile, listed as one); leave empty kinds out of the index (chosen).
- **Reason:** Search Console should show errors only for things that are wrong, so a real one is not lost among them. The end to end test now checks that places joins the index once a place is worth indexing.

## D-262 | 2026-10-14 | A badge is sent with its photo, and its number is digits | Product owner, Claude
- **Found:** by the product owner on onboarding's badge step, on a phone: with a photo attached, Continue said "Check the highlighted field" and highlighted nothing; with no photo, it carried on. Since D-224 a browser that cannot write WebP stores the picture as PNG, which the badges bucket accepts, but `submit_verification` still required the path to end in `.webp`, so the submission was refused, and the field it named was never put on the screen.
- **Decision:** `submit_verification` accepts a `.webp` or `.png` photo, and needs one: the photo sent with it, or one still on file from before. A decision clears the photo (D-158), so sending again after one needs a new photo. The form asks for the photo before sending, beside the photo, at the same time as anything else that is missing, and every field the database refuses is shown beside that field (`submitBadge` maps the field it names). Skipping the step still sends nothing.
- **The number:** digits, five to seven of them, with spaces dropped, in the form and in the database, replacing D-056's 4 to 12 letters or digits. The product owner asked for a check on the number's length. ADI badges carry six digits; GOV.UK does not publish the format, so one either side allows for a badge numbered before or after today's, while letters, or the eleven digits of a phone number or the sixteen characters of a driving licence, are refused with "Enter the number on your badge in digits, like 123456". A person still checks the number against the register (D-056).
- **Options:** Exactly six digits (would turn away any badge numbered otherwise, which nobody would see happening); keep D-056's shape (the product owner asked for more); five to seven digits (chosen).
- **From the profile:** the product owner's "major problem": a refused badge showed "Not approved" and nothing else. The badge card now shows why it was refused and a "Send your badge again" button, which opens the same form in a sheet; "Send your badge" does the same for a badge skipped in onboarding, and "Send your new badge" for an approved badge that has run out, when the profile has stopped taking bookings anyway (`canSendBadge`). Not while one is being checked, and not over an approved badge still in date: sending sets the profile back to "Being checked", which takes away the tick and the booking link until a person has looked. Renewing a badge ahead of its expiry without losing the tick in between needs a state of its own (the old badge still counts while the new one is checked) and is not built yet. The badge warning tells an instructor to send the new one once the old one runs out, which is what the profile allows.

## D-263 | 2026-10-14 | Onboarding goes back a screen, and carries on in order from there | Product owner, Claude
- **Found:** by the product owner: going back from step 3 to step 2 and pressing Continue went to step 4. Saving or skipping a screen moved on from the furthest screen reached, which is stored on the profile, rather than from the screen being shown. There was also no way back except the browser's.
- **Decision:** Every screen after the first has a Back link to the screen before it in the person's own list (an instructor at a school has no prices screen, so their hours go back to the area). Saving or skipping carries on to the screen after the one being shown (`afterStep`). What is stored as reached never goes backwards, so the screens already done stay open, and finishing the last screen finishes onboarding as before. Skip names its screen to the server, which checks it is one they can open.
- **Options:** Keep jumping to the furthest screen (what the product owner reported as wrong); go back to the furthest screen after an edit; carry on in order (chosen).
- **Reason:** Somebody who goes back has something to change on that screen and the ones after it, and the numbers on the progress bar say the next one is next.

## D-264 | 2026-10-14 | Handing over to Stripe does not refresh the page first | Claude
- **Found:** the product owner reported that "Set up payments" and "Finish setting up" showed an error page for a moment before Stripe's page loaded. Both actions, and the one that opens the Pro checkout, asked Next to refresh the screen (`revalidatePath`) just before the browser left for Stripe. On the live site that refresh asks Stripe for the account again as it draws, so it was still arriving when the browser left, and the app has no error screen of its own to show instead of Next's.
- **Decision:** `connectPayments` and `startProCheckout` no longer refresh anything: the screen is read afresh when somebody comes back (the return addresses are rendered on demand), and there is nothing to refresh on a page being left.
- **Not reproduced:** the fake payments provider answers at once, so locally the refresh always finished first and nothing flashed, even with Stripe's delay added by hand. If it still happens after this, a screenshot of it will say what it is.

## D-265 | 2026-10-14 | The terms and privacy notice are agreed to with a tick, on the record | Product owner, Claude
- **Asked for:** the product owner: users were not asked to accept the privacy policy and terms, and it should be mandatory. Signing up only said "By creating an account you agree to our terms and privacy notice", with no tick, no links and nothing written down.
